# Sendivogius
<note>Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636) was a world-renowned Polish alchemist, philosopher, and medical doctor. He is most famous for his theories on the "nitre of the air" (a precursor to the discovery of oxygen) and his influential alchemical treatises, which were studied by figures like Isaac Newton.</note>

-># THE SENDIVOGIAN ALCHEMICAL TRIPOD,<-
<note>original: "TRIPVS CHIMICVS SENDIVOGIANVS"</note>
->## A Triple Alchemical Jewel.<-
->That is:<-

->**I.**<-
->**Twelve Treatises**<-
->concerning the **Philosopher’s Stone**<-
<note>The Philosopher's Stone was the legendary substance of alchemy, capable of turning base metals into gold and granting eternal life or perfect health.</note>
->of the ancient sages, in which its<-
->origin, preparation, and completion<-
->are shown and demonstrated as bright and clear<-
->from the light of nature, in a manner<-

->**II. An Elegant and Ingenious Dialogue**<-
->between an Alchemist, **Mercury**,<-
->and Nature; wherein the most hidden secret<-
->of the Stone—the **Philosophers' Mercury**—is painted<-
->and described in its true, permanent colors.<-

->**III. A Treatise and Dialogue on Sulfur**,<-
->the other principal component of the <term>Tincture</term>, which<-
<note><term>Tincture</term>: In alchemical practice, a "tincture" refers to a potent liquid or solid extract—often synonymous with the Philosopher's Stone—capable of transmuting metals or curing diseases.</note>
->discovers and reveals the most secret<-
->mysteries of Nature.<-

->Originally written in Latin by a highly enlightened, very learned,<-

->the first time with great diligence by<-
->**Hisaias Sub Cruce, Ath:**<-

<meta>This page serves as the Latin title page for the collection, providing the academic credentials for the work. While the previous page emphasized the German translation for "lovers of the Art," this page presents the formal Latin titles of the three core treatises by Michael Sendivogius, noting their linguistic journey from Italian into Latin.</meta>

-># THE SENDIVOGIAN ALCHEMICAL TRIPOD<-
<note>original: "TRIPUS CHIMICUS SENDIVOGIANUS." A "tripod" refers to a three-legged stand, symbolizing the three distinct treatises contained in this volume.</note>
->### by Michael Sendivogius<-
<note>Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636) was a prominent Polish alchemist, diplomat, and court physician. He is best known for his theories on the existence of a "food of life" in the air (later identified as oxygen) and his influential alchemical writings.</note>
->That is<-
->**A Compendium of Treatises**<-

->In which are contained:<-

->**I. Concerning the Philosopher’s Stone**, which is called "Golden," and in which the true and correct path for finding it is discussed with the utmost brevity.<-

->**II. Concerning Sulfur**, which is the other principle of all metals.<-
<note>In the "Mercury-Sulfur theory" of alchemy, Sulfur represented the active, masculine, and combustible principle of matter, while Mercury represented the passive and fluid principle.</note>

->**III. Concerning Nature**, that is, the generation of all things.<-

->All things converted from the Italian into the Latin language, and now for the first time published.<-
<note>original: "Omnia ex Italico in Latinum sermonem conversa." This suggests that while the author was Polish, these specific versions of the texts may have circulated in Italian manuscripts before being formalized in Latin for the scholarly community.</note>

->Strasbourg,<-
<note>original: "Argentorati," the Latin name for the city of Strasbourg, which was a major center for alchemical publishing in the 17th century.</note>
->At the House of the Heirs of Eberhard Zetzner.<-
<note>The Zetzner family were famous printers; Eberhard Zetzner published the *Theatrum Chemicum*, the largest collection of alchemical texts ever printed.</note>
->1628.<-

<summary>This is the Latin title page for a collection of three influential alchemical works by Michael Sendivogius, detailing his theories on the Philosopher's Stone, Sulfur, and the laws of Nature. It establishes the book's scholarly lineage, noting its translation from Italian into Latin and its publication in Strasbourg in 1628.</summary>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page continues from the Latin title page of "The Sendivogian Alchemical Tripod." Having established the formal academic credentials and the contents of the volume (treatises on the Philosopher’s Stone, Sulfur, and Nature), the book now transitions into a German-language preface addressed directly to the reader. This shift into the vernacular suggests a desire to reach both the scholar and the "lover of the Art" who may not be as fluent in Latin.</meta>

**Foreword to the**
**Alchemical Reader.**

[Decorative initial D]ecause, according to the common proverb, good wares require no praise—or, in the manner of the poets, good wine needs no hoop <note>original: "Raiffs." This refers to the metal or wooden hoop of a wine barrel.</note> or displayed ivy wreath <note>In historical Europe, an ivy bush or wreath hung outside a tavern was a traditional sign that good wine was served within. The author is saying the book's quality is so high it needs no advertising.</note>—it seems entirely unnecessary to introduce many boasts and words of praise for this little book in this preface. For the work speaks well enough for itself and is renowned and well-known throughout most of Europe among both the learned and the lovers of this secret philosophy <note>original: "haimlichen Philosophy," a standard term for alchemy, which was considered the "occult" or "hidden" study of nature's inner workings.</note>.

This can be recognized partly by the fact that it has been reprinted so often, in so many different places, and in so many various formats (which is, admittedly, a minor argument, considering that many counterfeit and even annoying pamphlets <note>original: "Chartecken," a derogatory term for cheap, poorly made books or scandalous broadsheets.</note> are often published). But it is recognized more significantly because it is highly praised, approved, and cited by the most excellent philosophers of our time. But most of all 
**A ij but** <note>"A ij" is a signature mark used by early modern printers to help binders assemble the pages in the correct order. The text "but most of all" anticipates the argument on the following page.</note>

<summary>This preface introduces the Sendivogian treatises by arguing that their reputation among European scholars and alchemists is so established that they require no formal endorsement or "advertising." The author notes that the frequent reprinting and citations by prominent philosophers serve as proof of the work's enduring value.</summary>

**but**—that it shows the true and clear
**Light of Nature** <note>The "Light of Nature" (*Lumen Naturae*) is a central concept in alchemical thought, referring to the innate wisdom or divine spark within the physical world that allows humans to understand the mysteries of creation through observation and reason.</note>, and with that same light,
ignites a flame in the most hidden corners and inner-

with the little talent <note>original: "pfündlin." This is a reference to the "talents" or "pounds" in the biblical parable (Matthew 25:14-30), meaning the specific gifts or abilities God has given an individual.</note> granted to him by God,
follow his other calling all the more diligently.

<meta>This page continues the "Vorrede" (Preface). The previous page established that the "Light of Nature" and the "Key of the Art" are gifts from God, intended only for those destined by fate to understand them. This page expands on that idea by explaining that God created a diversity of human talents and inclinations, just as He created a diversity of species in nature, justifying why some are drawn to the "subtle art" of alchemy while others pursue different labors.</meta>

->### Preface.<-

For just as GOD the Almighty
did not want to create only one star,
one bird, one fish, one animal,
one herb, and one mineral, but 
rather of every kind many thousands 
of types—indeed, innumerable, incomprehensible, 
and unfathomably manifold—so He 
has much less wanted to have humans 
be of only one kind and skilled in only 
one business; rather, it is His order, 
will, and command, that one in one thing, 
another in another, practices his 
delight, knowledge, and **art** <note>original: "kunſt." In this period, "art" referred to any specialized skill, craft, or branch of knowledge requiring study and practice.</note>. Therefore, 
one builds above the Earth into the heights 
above himself—high houses, towers, 
and pyramids, like the storks; 
the other burrows in the Earth, 
in the ground and soil with planting, 
sowing, and setting of trees, fields, 
and **pleasure gardens** <note>original: "Luſtgärtẽ." These were ornamental gardens designed for enjoyment and the display of exotic plants, often featuring symbolic or philosophical layouts.</note>, like the moles; 
the third builds under the Earth, 
toward the abyss or (in **mining jargon** <note>original: "Berg-welſch." Literally "mountain-foreign," this refers to the specialized technical language or cant used by miners which was often unintelligible to outsiders.</note>) 
the **Eternal Deep** <note>original: "Ewigen tieffe." A poetic and technical term for the profound depths of a mine where the most valuable ores were believed to mature.</note>, into the 
bottomless mines, like a **wren** <note>original: "Königlin," literally "little king," the common German name for the wren. Used here as a metaphor for a small creature that nonetheless navigates complex or hidden spaces.</note>. 
Thus one practices subtle arts, 
the other hard manual labor: 
one sustains himself in the earth, 
the other in the water, the third in the fire, 
yet all in the air; <note>This is an allusion to the four classical elements—Earth, Water, Fire, and Air—suggesting that while humans may labor in different elemental domains, they all share a common vital medium.</note> for thus it must <margin><unclear>faint, illegible handwritten notes</unclear></margin>

reason why so few in

<meta>This page continues the "Preface" from the previous page, completing the second reason why many fail in alchemy—due to a lack of spiritual and practical preparation. It then introduces the third reason for the book's publication: its exceptional clarity compared to other texts. Finally, it warns that despite this clarity, the secrets are still veiled to protect them from the unworthy, citing biblical authority.</meta>

->### Preface.<-

...approach it with **unwashed senses**, <note>original: "ohngewaschenen Sinnen." A metaphor for those who are spiritually or intellectually unprepared, lacking the necessary purity of intent or "cleanliness" of soul required for sacred work.</note> hearts, and hands; whereas before all things, what is required here is a pure **fear of God**, true knowledge of Nature, and diligent **labor in the fire**. <note>original: "arbeit im Fewr." This refers to the practical, physical work of the alchemist at the furnace—the "hand-work" or *labora*—which must accompany the prayerful *ora*.</note>

And then thirdly, to properly illustrate for the **art-seeking reader** <note>original: "kunstbegirigen Leser." One who is eager to learn the Hermetic Art or alchemy.</note> the excellence of this little book—as one that is perfect in its teaching, exceedingly rich in understanding, abundant in maxims, yet incomparable in clarity when compared to other **chemical** <note>original: "Chimischen." In the 17th century, "chemical" and "alchemical" were used interchangeably to describe the study of matter, the transmutation of metals, and the preparation of spagyric medicines.</note> writings.

However, let no one imagine that everything herein is described so orderly, according to every syllable and letter—pre-chewed and fully illustrated. For such would be against the command of God, who says: <margin>Matthew 7:6 — Do not give what is holy to the dogs, nor cast your pearls before swine.</margin> "Do not throw that which is holy before the dogs, or pearls before the swine, lest they trample them underfoot," or turn them against you.

It would also be against the honor of this **Art**—for whatever becomes common is dishonored—and against the custom, will, and intention of all the **Sages**, <note>original: "Weisen." This refers to the "Philosophers" or master alchemists of history, such as Hermes Trismegistus or Geber, who traditionally used riddles and allegories to hide their secrets from the profane.</note> who in all their writings...

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the Preface, moving from a scriptural warning against revealing secrets to a series of vivid metaphors that define the book’s role. It frames the text not as a complete encyclopedia, but as an essential interpretive "key" or "spectacle" needed to understand the broader, often-obscure body of alchemical literature.</meta>

->### Preface.<-

...curse and damn the one who improperly reveals anything of this secret to the unworthy. Likewise, let no one allow himself to dream that this book contains everything within itself, or that he would have no need to read others: not in the least. 

Rather, this treatise is like **spectacles** for weak eyesight; like a **lit candle** in a dark house; like a **compass needle** pointing to the Pole on the wild, unknown sea; like a **divining rod** <note>original: "glückruth." A tool traditionally used to locate hidden ores or water; here, it symbolizes the book's ability to find hidden truths.</note> for hidden metal; like a **door** into the **Garden of the Hesperides**; <note>In Greek mythology, this garden grew golden apples. Alchemists frequently used this as a metaphor for the search for the "Golden" result of the Great Work.</note> like a **key** to a locked chamber; like a **ladder** to the high **Sun-Tree**; <note>original: "Sonnenbaum." A common alchemical illustration representing the final, "solar" stage of the process—the creation of gold or the Philosopher’s Stone.</note> like a **bridge** over the dangerous **Silver-Sea**; <note>original: "Silber-Meer." This refers to the "Mercurial" or "Lunar" stage of alchemy, often described as a volatile or "sea-like" state of matter that must be crossed to reach stability.</note> indeed, like a true guide to the correct understanding of **philosophical, allegorical, and chemical** <note>original: "Chimischen." In this era, chemistry and alchemy were not yet distinct disciplines; the term encompassed both laboratory practice and spiritual theory.</note> writings.

For the sake of such many and manifold uses, it has been deemed advisable that—just as with the twelve little treatises <note>Likely referring to the "Twelve Treatises" of Michael Sendivogius, a famous alchemical work of the period.</note> previously—so now also these two beautiful discourses on **Mercury** <note>original: "Mercurio."</note> and **Sulfur** <note>original: "Schwefel." Mercury and Sulfur were considered the two primary principles or "parents" of all metals in alchemical theory.</note> from the
Latin

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the Preface, shifting from the metaphors for the book’s utility to the practical matters of its translation and the identity of its author. It specifically addresses the transition from the original Latin into German and provides clues to the author's identity through famous alchemical anagrams.</meta>

->### Preface.<-

...translated from Latin into the German language and rendered well; we believe this has been accomplished here to the best of our ability.

Finally, concerning the author of this treatise, it is sufficiently evident from the titles and the introduction of his writings, where he explicitly states that he does not wish to be known—especially while he is still alive—for he has more than once faced extreme danger on account of this art. <note>The "art" of alchemy was a dangerous profession; successful alchemists were often kidnapped or imprisoned by monarchs desperate for gold.</note> Nevertheless, he has revealed his name in two different rearrangements of letters <note>These are anagrams, a common method for alchemists to claim their work without revealing their identity to the public.</note>: namely in the treatise on *Mercury*, with the words:
"He who loves the race of the divine Lechite" <note>original: "qui DIVI LESCHI genus amat." This Latin anagram points to the author Michael Sendivogius, who was Polish—the "Lechites" being a historical name for the Polish people.</note>;
And in the other treatise on *Sulphur*:
"Angel, teach me the law" <note>original: "Angelus doce mihi jus." This is another Latin anagram for Michael Sendivogius.</note>;
which, in his honor, may also be interpreted in this way:
"I will send, as I am able." <note>original: "Wyl ſenden / ſo ich mag." A German phonetic play or anagram on the name "Sendivogius."</note>

Moreover, recently an impudent deceiver and scoundrel, under the hidden name **A. O. E.**, published a half-
<note>The catchword at the bottom "A v teutſch-" indicates the sentence continues on the next page, likely referring to a German translation or edition.</note>

<note>In the bottom right corner, there are handwritten notes and a small circular stamp that are illegible due to the age of the paper and the quality of the imprint.</note>

<summary>This page discusses the translation of the work from Latin to German and identifies the author, Michael Sendivogius, through his famous Latin and German anagrams. It also begins a warning against a contemporary "deceiver" writing under a pseudonym.</summary>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the Preface, specifically addressing a "German-half Latin" commentary on Sendivogius's work. The translator defends their own decision to name the author explicitly, contrasting their respectful approach with a previous, low-quality edition that allegedly misrepresented the author's original ideas and discouraged him from publishing further works.</meta>

->### Preface.<-

German-half Latin exposition on the twelve small treatises <note>This refers to the *Novum Lumen Chymicum* (New Light of Chemistry), a foundational alchemical text consisting of twelve treatises.</note> (which he does not understand in the slightest point, nor will he ever understand so long as he remains in his filthiness) [which he has] spread abroad, and also explicitly set the name of the noble **Lord von Sendwog** <note>Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636), a famous Polish alchemist and diplomat.</note> upon it: also there is almost no one who does not arrange these letters <note>A reference to the anagrams mentioned on the previous page that reveal Sendivogius's identity.</note> in this way: Therefore, neither he nor anyone else will be able to blame me for placing the name over his own work—even though it appears in a different dress <note>The "dress" refers to the style or linguistic presentation of the text.</note> and a foreign language (which is yet not foreign to him). For this was done in his honor, and not like that other version, which was done to his dishonor: as I have indeed, in truth, along with others, recently heard one of his good acquaintances here lamenting greatly that his meaning had been so distorted, corrupted, and shamed—even secretly suggesting that he had not created the 12 Treatises through his own invention, but had taken them from old figures <note>In this context, "figures" refers to alchemical diagrams or symbolic illustrations.</note>: this may well also provide a reason why his third dialogue on salt has not been published.

Was

<summary>The translator defends naming Michael Sendivogius as the author, arguing that a previous poor-quality edition had slandered the alchemist by suggesting his work was plagiarized from old diagrams.</summary>
<keywords>Michael Sendivogius, Twelve Treatises, alchemy, plagiarism, translation, Salt, Novum Lumen Chymicum</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the Preface. The previous page discussed the mistreatment of Sendivogius's work by incompetent editors. Here, the author warns against "land-betrayers" (swindlers) who publish false alchemical books, leading honest seekers into ruin, and directs the reader toward the study of Nature itself.</meta>

->**Preface.**<-

What reward such intentional swindlers and deceivers of the people may one day expect is not difficult to conclude. In truth, they are the great seal and padlock <note>The original German uses the term *Mahlſchloß*, literally a "mill-lock," signifying a metaphorical barrier that stops the "mill" of alchemical work from functioning.</note> of this art; for through the countless number of false books and entire "theaters" <note>This refers to *Theatra*, the massive printed compendiums of the 17th century that collected hundreds of different alchemical treatises into single, often confusing, volumes.</note> full of dreams (of which I could name a long list, even just those well known to me), many aspiring seekers of truth are shamefully led into the dark <note>The original phrase *hinder das Liecht geführet* (led behind the light) is a German idiom meaning to be deceived or hoodwinked.</note>. While they trust such boasters, they set aside authentic books, abandon the right path of Nature, spend their lives wandering in labyrinths, and finally, out of grief, end their lives miserably in hunger and sorrow.

But he who can become truly wise from the misfortune of others is indeed a skillful man. He may let go of these uninvited new interpreters of dreams and, for a true interpretation, apply himself to the great Book of the World <note>The *Liber Mundi* or "Book of the World" is the philosophical idea that the natural world is a text written by God, which the scientist or alchemist must learn to read.</note> and the four leaves of the elements. There, on one side, is written **Action** <note>original: *Actio*.</note>, and on the other, **Passion** <note>original: *Paſſio*. In early modern physics and alchemy, "Passion" refers to the capacity of a substance to be acted upon or changed by an external force, the necessary counterpart to "Action."</note>; alongside these, however, he should apply the proven <note>The text cuts off here with the catch-word "proven," which will lead into a list of reliable authors on the following page.</note>

<summary>The author warns against fraudulent alchemical "theaters" and false books that lead seekers to ruin, advising students to study the "Book of Nature" and the fundamental elements instead.</summary>

# Preface.

...[consulted those] experienced in the **Alchemical**, **Mining**, **Smelting**, **Refining**, and **Parting** arts; since this is the ABCs, which one must know perfectly, backward and forward, before one intends to undertake this work. For just as with letters, one first begins to form syllables and finally to read. For how shall an inexperienced person understand the saying which commands him to dissolve <term>solve <note>The Latin *solvere* (to dissolve or loosen) refers here to the alchemical process of breaking down a substance into its fundamental components.</note></term>, if he has not previously, often and well, dissolved all kinds of metals, minerals, stones, bones <note>original: *Bain*</note>, and other growths? It would happen to him as it does to a young boy, who must first look up in his primer <note>original: *Namenbuch*—literally a "name book" used for learning the names of letters and basic spelling.</note> to see what kind of letter this might be.

As for the rest, if anything has not been made according to everyone's sense, opinion, and thought, may he change it for the best and in the best way; being assured that it has all been done out of a good spirit, a faithful heart, and a well-meaning will, for his pleasure, honor, and benefit. Recommending myself herewith to the same in the best form.

Written in **Freudenstadt** <note>Literally "Joyous City," a town in the Black Forest founded in 1599, which was a significant center for mining and silver production during this era.</note>, under the protection of **Parnassus** <note>A reference to Mount Parnassus, the mythological home of the Muses, suggesting the work was written under intellectual or poetic inspiration.</note>, on **Michaelmas Day** <note>September 29th, a major feast day in the Christian calendar often used as a date for settling accounts and contracts.</note>, in the year 1627, according to the small, common, and shorter count <note>original: "627." It was a common 17th-century convention to omit the "1000" when writing the date.</note>.

# Preface

<margin>
A sincere warning not to consider the outward letter alone, but to take heed of Nature and her workings.
</margin>

...[the right] path, which Nature shows us in all her workings, following it with diligence.

I therefore wish to remind the sincere reader that he should understand my writings not only according to the **outward letter** <note>The "outward letter" refers to the literal text or superficial instructions, which the author warns can be misleading without an understanding of the underlying natural principles.</note>, but according to what Nature is capable of, so that he does not have to regret and lament his time, effort, labor, and expenses in vain and to his own harm. 

He should consider that this is a **science and Art of the Wise** <note>A traditional term for alchemy, implying it is a sacred knowledge reserved for those with both intellect and moral integrity.</note>, and not for the unskilled; and that the meaning of the **Philosophers** <note>In this context, "Philosophers" refers to the ancient and medieval masters of alchemy whose writings were often encoded in allegory.</note> is of a far different nature than can be grasped or understood either by loud-mouthed boasters, or by learned, conceited mockers, nor by those who are consumed by vices against their conscience, and since they cannot advance through any virtue, they undertake to achieve it through roguery and the slandering of honest people; nor finally by inexperienced vagabonds, who with their **whitening** <note>original: *dealbationi*; a Latin-derived technical term referring to the alchemical process of "whitening" or purifying a substance to reach the *albedo* stage of the Great Work.</note>

# Twelve Little Treatises or
# Chapters on the Philosophers’

## The First Treatise.

### On Nature / what it is / and how those should be prepared / who wish to fathom it.

<note>A large, decorative woodcut initial letter "E" marks the beginning of the text.</note> MANY wise and learned people— <margin>This art flourished before and after the Great Flood.</margin>
many hundreds of years ago / indeed, even before the Great Flood / as Hermes <note>original: *Hermes*. This refers to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure to whom many foundational alchemical and hermetic texts are attributed.</note> testifies / wrote much about the preparation of the <term>Philosophers' Stone<gloss>original: *Stain der Weysen*. A legendary alchemical substance capable of turning base metals into noble ones and acting as an elixir of life.</gloss></term> / and left behind so many writings for us / that / if NATURE did not work such things every single day / which are truly to be believed / almost no one would believe any longer / that there is a NATURE / because in olden times there were not as many people / who invented new things. And indeed / our <margin>Nature and its operations are to be observed above all else.</margin>
ancestors, the dear ancients / considered nothing else / but Nature / and what is possible for Nature / and although they remained upon the simple and plain path and course of Nature / they nonetheless discovered such things / which we in the present time / who have such a great number of many things / could hardly have imagined or conceived in our minds. This now happens / because Nature / and the birth of all things in the world / appears simple and contemptible to us / wherefore we then also with our Spirit—

B

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the "First Treatise," contrasting the sophisticated lab techniques of modern "chymists" with the simpler, yet more successful, natural philosophy of the ancients.</meta>

->On the Philosophers’ Stone 3<-

...reached the highest degree. For the alchemical art has discovered such subtle things that one could hardly invent anything higher; and there is such a difference between it and the ancient philosophers, just as between a watchmaker and a common blacksmith. For although they both work with iron, neither understands the work of the other, even though each is a master in his own art. If today the father of all philosophers, Hermes <note>original: "Hermes". Referring to Hermes Trismegistus.</note>, and the ingenious Geber <note>original: "Geber". Geber is the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, an 8th-century polymath considered a founder of chemistry.</note>, together with the deep-thinking Raymond Lully <note>original: "Raymund Lullio". A 13th-century Majorcan philosopher and mystic to whom many alchemical works were later attributed.</note>, were to rise again, they would not be regarded as philosophers, but as students by the <term>Chymists<gloss>In this era, "chymistry" referred to the practical laboratory work of alchemy, focusing on the manipulation of substances.</gloss></term>. For they would know nothing of so much <term>Distillation<gloss>A process of separating substances through heating and cooling to collect vapors.</gloss></term>, <term>Circulation<gloss>The continuous redistillation of a liquid in a closed vessel, often intended to "purify" the substance.</gloss></term>, <term>Calcination<gloss>The reduction of a solid to a powder or ash through intense heat.</gloss></term>, and other countless works of the artists, which people in the present time have found and devised from their books. 

One thing alone is lacking to us, which we should know: what they were capable of and what they made—namely the <term>Philosophers’ Stone<gloss>original: "Philosophischē Stain".</gloss></term> or natural <term>Tincture<gloss>original: "Tinctur". A powerful alchemical substance capable of "dyeing" or transforming the quality of a metal or medicine.</gloss></term>. While we pursue this, we discover other things; and it would be no wonder—if the procreation of mankind were not so common and Nature did not maintain its rights so firmly therein—that failure would occur even in that regard <note>The author suggests that if human biology weren't so resiliently managed by Nature, modern experimenters would probably manage to disrupt that as well.</note>.

But so that I may return to my purpose: I have promised that in this first treatise I wish to explain what Nature is, so that no vain delusion [leads] us from the simple...

<summary>The author argues that while modern alchemists have developed complex laboratory techniques unknown to the ancients, they still fail to produce the Philosophers' Stone. He proposes to return to the study of "Nature" as the foundation of the work.</summary>

For we seek the spirit in the body, and the life in the dead, and the light in the darkness; yet the Stone of the Wise is a true spirit, a true life, and a true light.

### On the Erroneous Ways of Modern Chymists

For although the present-day **Chymists** possess great skill in their hands, they lack the true ground and the light of nature, which the ancients possessed in abundance. The ancients did not have so many instruments, nor such complex furnaces, nor so many vessels of glass and clay as we have now; but they had the knowledge of the <term>First Matter<gloss>original: "Materia Prima." In alchemical theory, this is the fundamental, undifferentiated substance from which all physical matter originates.</gloss></term>, which is the beginning and the end of all things.

Therefore, whoever wishes to find it must seek it in its own nature and not in foreign things. For the Stone is not found in herbs, nor in animals, nor in the common elements of the earth, as many falsely believe who spend their gold and silver on such useless labors.

<margin>The Stone is not in herbs or animals.</margin>

They search through all of nature but ignore the very thing that is closest to them. They act like someone who seeks a horse while they are already sitting upon its back.

<note>This "man on a horse" metaphor is a recurring theme in alchemical literature, suggesting that the secret substance needed for the Stone is common, inexpensive, and often overlooked by those seeking complexity.</note>

## The First Treatise
### Concerning the Great Stone of the Ancients

# On the Stone of the Wise. 5

In alchemical terms, "volatile" refers to a substance or force that easily evaporates or moves, representing the active, spiritual essence within physical matter.</gloss></term> who performs her office within bodies, and has her seat and place in the will of God. For us, in this matter, she is useful for nothing else than that we know her place and location, which are most fitting and convenient for her. This means that we know how to unite one thing with another according to Nature, so that no person is united with wood, nor a bull or other beast with a metal, but that each works and acts within its own kind; then Nature will also do her part. <margin>Nature is invisible; she is a volatile spirit. She exists in the will of God.</margin>

Nature, as mentioned above, has no other place or dwelling than in the will of God. Those who wish to fathom Nature must be constituted as Nature herself is: namely truthful, **simple** <note>The original German "schlecht" here means simple, plain, or straightforward, rather than "bad" as in modern German.</note>, patient, steadfast, etc., and most importantly, God-fearing and harmless to their neighbor. <margin>How the researchers of Nature must be constituted.</margin>

# The First Treatise on the Stone of the Wise.

Take heed of what and through what she [Nature] is improved; thus it will always be able to happen or be made in its own kind. For example: if you desire to bring a metal (as is our intention) to a higher **virtue** <term>virtue<gloss>original: "tugendt." In early modern natural philosophy, this refers to the inherent power, potency, or specific quality of a substance.</gloss></term> than Nature has done, you must take a metallic nature and place it into **male and female** <note>In alchemical theory, "male and female" refer to the two opposing but complementary principles—often identified as Sulfur and Mercury—that must be combined to generate the Philosopher's Stone.</note>; otherwise you will accomplish nothing. For if you wished to make the virtue of metal out of herbs, you would work in vain, just as you cannot make wood out of a dog or another animal.

## The Second Treatise.

### Concerning the operation and working of Nature in our intended **seed** <note>original: "Spermate, oder saat." The author uses both the Latin and German terms to describe the creative essence or "sperm" of matter.</note>.

<note>A decorative initial letter E marks the start of this chapter.</note> Now I have taught that Nature is one, true, everywhere visible or apparent, **continuous** <note>original: "continua"</note> or joined together, which is recognized from the things she brings forth, such as wood, herbs, etc. Also that a researcher of Nature should be truthful, **simple** <note>original: "schlecht." In this period, the word meant "plain," "honest," or "straightforward" rather than its modern meaning of "bad."</note>, patient, and steadfast, who directs his thoughts and mind only toward one thing alone, etc. Now we must speak of the working of Nature. Just as Nature exists in the will of God, and God created her, or incorporated her into every **imagination** <note>original: "Einbildung." In the context of 16th and 17th-century alchemy, this refers to the "formative power" or the internal blueprint that directs a substance to take its physical form.</note>, so also has Nature made a seed for herself; that is, her will within the elements.

<summary>This page establishes the alchemical principle that one must work within the specific "kind" of a substance to improve it, specifically requiring metallic "male and female" principles for metallic transmutation. **The Second Treatise. 7**

<margin>What the seed or sower of Nature is, and how it is constituted.</margin>

...ments. This [Nature] is indeed but a single one, and yet it brings forth entirely different things; but she works nothing without her **seed** <term>seed<gloss>original: "Sperma." In this context, it refers to the generative essence or "starting material" of a substance, rather than biological sperm.</gloss></term>. Whatever the seed wills, that Nature works; for she is just like a master craftsman’s instrument or tool. Accordingly, the seed of every thing is better and more useful to the artist than Nature herself. For out of Nature, without the seed, you can accomplish as much as a goldsmith can out of gold or silver without fire, or a farmer without seed. When one has the seed, Nature will soon find her way, be it for evil or for good. She works in the seed as God works in man’s free will; and this is a great miracle: that Nature obeys the seed, not by force, but of her own accord, just as God grants everything that man wills, not by compulsion, but out of His own will. Therefore He has given man a free will, whether for good or for evil. 

Thus the seed is now an **elixir** <term>elixir<gloss>A preparation able to change or "perfect" a substance.</gloss></term> of every thing, or its **fifth essence** <term>fifth essence<gloss>original: "fünfftes Wesen," referring to the Quintessence—the purest and most potent form of a substance.</gloss></term>, or its most perfect **decoction** <term>decoction<gloss>The process of boiling a substance to extract its essence.</gloss></term> and **digestion** <term>digestion<gloss>In alchemy, a process where a substance is subjected to gentle heat over a long period to mature it.</gloss></term>, or a **balm of sulfur** <note>original: "Balsam des Schwefels." The fundamental vital fluid or moisture thought to sustain life and the integrity of metals.</gloss></term> in metals. **The Second Treatise**

<margin>The metallic seed or sperm, [and] how it is generated.</margin>

<margin>What the center of the earth is.</margin>

...imagination or internal formative power of Nature. Just as a man's seed has its center, or the vessel for his seed in the kidneys, so also the four elements, through their perpetual **motion** <term>motion<gloss>original: "motum." In alchemical physics, this refers to the continuous movement or "churning" of the elements that drives all natural change.</gloss></term> (each according to its quality and kind), cast a seed into the center of the earth, where it is **digested** <term>digested<gloss>original: "digerirt." An alchemical term for a process of slow, gentle heating and maturation, likened to the way the body processes food.</gloss></term> and cast out by that motion. The center of the earth, however, is an empty place where nothing can remain at rest. Into the **outer center** <term>outer center<gloss>original: "excentrum." The author's term for the boundary layer surrounding the absolute center of the Earth.</gloss></term> (as I call it), or into the border and outer circumference of the center, the four elements pour their qualities, just as a man [casts] his seed into the **womb** <note>original: "Mutter," which literally means "mother" but was the standard Early Modern German term for the uterus.</note> of a woman, in which nothing of the seed remains; rather, after the womb has received its due portion, she casts out the rest. In the same way, it happens in the center of the earth that the **magnetic force** <note>The author suggests that different locations in the earth have a "magnetism" for specific minerals, explaining why gold is found in some places and lead in others.</note> of a place draws to itself a thing that is suited to it for giving birth; the rest is cast out to become stones and other superfluities. For all things have their origin in this same source, and nothing in this world is conceived except from this source.

As an example: Suppose there is a vessel with water on a flat table, placed in the middle of the table, and all sorts of things, all sorts of colors, as well as salt, etc., are laid around it, each one separately. When one then pours out such water in the middle, one sees that the same water distributes itself on all sides, and whichever part of it touches the red color, that same...

<summary>This page explains the generation of metals using a reproductive analogy, where the four elements "cast" a seed into the Earth's center to be matured. It introduces a "magnetic" theory of geology, where specific locations attract specific metallic qualities while rejecting waste material as common stone.</summary>

**Of the Workings of Nature. 9**

...the same thing becomes **Red**, which flows into the salt; it is salted by the same, and so on. For the Water does not change the places it reaches; rather, the places change the Water. <margin>The place where it is found. <note>original Latin: "Locus ubi reperitur."</note></margin>
In like manner, from the Center of the Earth comes the **Sperm** or **Seed**, which the four elements have poured into the Center. It flows into various places, and a thing is given its nature according to the Nature of its place. If it reaches a place of pure Water and Earth, then the thing also becomes pure. Of all things, there is but one kind of Seed or Sperm, and it brings forth various things, as appears from the following example:

<margin>There is only one seed for all things, but according to the difference of the places, it brings forth different things.</margin>

A man’s seed is a noble seed, created solely for the procreation of a Human being. But should a man misuse the same (which he can do out of his own free will), a **monstrosity** <note>original: "mißgeburt." In early modern science, this referred to "monstrous" births or biological abnormalities that were thought to prove certain natural laws.</note> would arise from it. For if—against the heavy and serious commandment of God—a man were to mix himself with a cow or another animal, an animal could easily receive such seed, for **Nature is one** <note>The author is arguing for a "Unitary Nature," the alchemical belief that all life and matter share a single underlying substance, making such transformations theoretically possible in their worldview.</note>. In such a case, no Human would result, but rather an animal and a monstrosity, because the seed had not encountered a place that was suitable and fitting for it. And through such inhuman and cursed mixing of Humans with animals, [monstrous] animals...

<summary>The author argues that there is only one universal "seed" for all things, and its final form—whether a specific metal or a living creature—is determined by the "place" or "womb" where it lands. To prove that the environment dictates the result, the text uses a contemporary (and graphic) argument about hybrid animals and the "unity of Nature."</summary>

# 19

## Of the Generation of All Kinds of Stones

...of **Sulfur** <note>original: *Sulphuris*. In alchemy, Sulfur represents the principle of combustibility, oiliness, and heat.</note> and comes to a place of pure **saltwater**, so **diamonds** are formed from it, and this occurs in the coldest places where that same **oiliness** <note>original: *fettigkeit*. This refers to the viscous, "fatty" quality of alchemical Sulfur which was thought to give substances their body and flammability.</note> cannot reach. For the oiliness would not permit this to happen. It is to be known that the **Spirit of the Water** <note>original: *Gaist deß Wassers*. This is the volatile, vaporous essence of water, purified of its heavy elemental nature.</note> is very light and is **sublimated** <term>sublimated</term> <note>The process of turning a solid into vapor through heat and then cooling it back into a solid; used here to describe how the earth's internal heat moves substances upward.</note> with gentle heat; but the oil and oiliness is only carried upward by places of great heat, for even when it is brought out from the **Center** <note>The *Centrum Mundi* or the center of the earth, believed to be the source of the "central fire" that drives all mineral growth.</note>, it congeals from even a slight cold and thus remains stationary. But the **Vapor** rises to its proper place and, in pure water, becomes small grains of stone.

<margin>
From where gemstones get their color.
</margin>

But how the **gemstones** attain their colors is to be known: this happens because of the **Sulfur** in the following manner. When the oiliness of the Sulfur is congealed or thickened through the aforementioned everlasting drive, then the **Spirit of the Water**, in its penetration, **digests**, cooks, and purifies the same through the power of **Salt** <note>original: *Saltz*. Salt is the third alchemical principle, representing stability, fixation, and the "body" of a substance.</note> until it is colored red or white by the digested color. This color, striving higher with that same **Spirit** <note>original: *Spiritu*.</note> because it has become more **subtle** <term>subtle</term> <note>In an alchemical sense, "subtle" means refined, thin, or spiritualized—the opposite of "gross" or "thick."</note>, is lifted on high with the Spirit through many repeated **distillations**. Thereafter, the Spirit has the power to penetrate into imperfect things, in which manner it also brings the color into them; which is afterward united with that same partly congealed or frozen water, and thus fills its passages, and finally through an inextinguishable...

Every thing is increased in its own **Seed**.

**On the Putrefaction of All Things. 21**

<margin>
The seed, what it is.
</margin>

For the **seed** <note>In alchemy, "seed" refers to the generative essence or "sperm" of a substance, believed to contain the blueprint for its growth.</note> is nothing other than a congealed **air** in a body, or a moist vapor; and if this is not resolved <term>resolved</term> <note>original: *resolviert*. The process of dissolving a solid back into a liquid or vaporous state.</note> by a similarly warm vapor, then it is of no use. 

<margin>
How and by what it is resolved.
</margin>

Therefore, the searchers of the **Art** <note>A common term for Alchemy, the "Great Art" of transformation.</note> must know what the seed is, so that they do not afterward seek a thing that does not exist; and they should understand that the same is of three kinds, which have arisen from the four elements. 

<margin>
Three types of seed.
1. Mineral.
2. Vegetable and
3. Animal.
</margin>

The first is the **Mineral**, of which we treat here; the second is the **Vegetable**; the third is the **Animal**. The Mineral seed is recognized by the **Philosophers** <note>original: *Philosophen*. Here, this refers specifically to the "Alchemical Philosophers" or masters of the craft.</note>; the Vegetable is common and known, as may be seen in fruits. The Animal is recognized through the intellect. 

<margin>
Winter, a cause of rotting.
</margin>

The Vegetable seed shows us how Nature creates it from the four elements; for it is to be known that **winter** is a cause of **putrefaction** <term>putrefaction</term> <note>original: *putrefaction*. The stage of decay or "blackening" where a substance is broken down so it can be reborn.</note>, since it suffocates the living spirits in the trees. But when these are resolved by the heat of the **sun** (in which there is an attractive **magnetic power** over all moistures), then the heat of Nature—which has been awakened by this impulse—drives a subtle vapor of water into the outer parts and circumference. 

<margin>
Origin of flowers, leaves, and branches.
</margin>

This then opens the air-holes of the tree and causes a trickling flow, ever separating the **pure** from the **impure**. Yet sometimes the pure precedes the impure; the pure yields and congeals into **flowers**, the impure into **leaves**,

...should have given, and would [unjustly] deny them the same. Are not metals held by God in the same dignity as trees? <margin>Nothing grows without seed, 1st tractate, page 7, at the end.</margin> One should hold this for certain: that nothing grows without a seed, for where there is no seed, a thing is dead. Thus, it must necessarily follow that the four elements either create a seed for the metals or bring them forth without a seed. If they come forth without a seed, they cannot be perfect, for every thing without a seed is imperfect, by reason of its <term>composite nature</term> <note>original: *compositi*. In alchemy, this refers to a substance made of combined elements; without a seed to organize them, the combination remains unstable or "imperfect."</note>. Whoever does not believe this indubitable truth is not worthy of exploring the secrets of Nature, for nothing comes forth in the world which does not have a seed.

The seed of metals is truly and actually planted within them, <margin>How the seed of metals is generated in the minerals.</margin> and its generation or growth proceeds in this way: The four elements, in the first operation and working of Nature, drip through the <term>Archeus</term> <note>The *Archeus* is a concept popularized by Paracelsus; it is the "inner alchemist" or vital force that directs the elements to form specific substances.</note> of Nature into the center of the earth a weighty vapor or mist of water, which is the seed of metals. <margin>Why it is generated as Mercury and then also Sulphur.</margin> This is called <term>Mercury</term> <note>In this context, Mercury is not the liquid metal in a thermometer, but the "Philosophical Mercury"—the fluid, feminine principle of all matter.</note> because of its fluidity and because it unites itself with every thing. Not because of its essence, but it is compared to <term>Sulphur</term> <note>Sulphur represents the masculine, fiery principle that gives form and "soul" to the Mercury.</note> because of its internal heat; and after its <term>congelation</term> <note>The process of a liquid or vapor becoming a solid material.</note>, it is the <term>radical moisture</term> <note>original: *humidum radicale*. The fundamental life-fluid believed to be the basis of all growth and existence.</note>, which one could call in German "such a moisture as was planted from the beginning of Nature." <margin>folio 7.</margin> And although the body of metals is [formed] out of this Mercury... <margin>It is a different Mercury.</margin>

34 ## The Tenth Treatise.

<margin>The difference between common Gold and Philosophical Gold.</margin>
Gold which is digested to the highest degree: for common gold is like a plant without seed; when it becomes ripe, it brings forth its seed; so also gold, when it ripens, gives a seed or **Tincture** <note>original: *Tinctur*; the concentrated essence or "medicine" of the Stone that allows for the transmutation of metals.</note>. However, someone might ask: why do gold or other metals not produce seeds? To this, one gives this reason: because they cannot become ripe due to the crude air; they do not have enough heat. And it happens in some places that one finds pure gold, which Nature intended to complete, but was hindered by the crude air.

<margin>Example.</margin>
As an example: we see that orange trees <note>original: *Pomerantzen Bäume*; referring to bitter oranges, which were exotic and difficult to grow in the colder climates of Northern Europe during this period.</note> certainly grow in our lands just like other trees. In Italy and those regions where they grow by Nature, they bear fruit because they have sufficient warmth. But in these cold places, they bear none at all; for when they should ripen, they remain stuck because they are overtaken by the cold, so that in these parts one can never have fruit from them according to Nature’s course alone,

<margin>Gently and with skill.</margin>
<note>original: *Suaviter & cum ingenio*. This is a central alchemical tenet: the master must work in harmony with natural laws, using intelligence rather than brute force.</note>
even though one occasionally comes to Nature's aid gently and skillfully, and in that way Art <note>In alchemy, "Art" refers to the human skill and laboratory practice used to perfect or accelerate what Nature began.</note> performs that which Nature could not have done. It happens in exactly the same way among the metals: gold can give fruit and seed, in which it multiplies itself, through the skill of an ingenious master who knows how to aid and advance Nature. But if he should attempt to perform it without Nature—
[to perform]

<summary>This page explains that while common gold is "sterile," it can be made to produce a "seed" or Tincture if ripened by the alchemist’s skill. Using the analogy of orange trees failing to fruit in cold climates, the author argues that the master must provide the "heat" and conditions that Nature lacks.</summary>

# On the Generation of the Son of the Seed 35

...accomplish <note>original: *verrichten*; this completes the thought from the previous page that if the alchemist does not work "gently and with skill," he will fail.</note>, then he would fail. For not only in this **Art** <note>original: *Kunst*; referring to Alchemy as the "Great Art" which assists natural processes.</note>, but in all other things, we can do nothing more than come to the aid of Nature, and this by no other means than through **fire** or heat. As to what kind of solution that should be, I wish to remind the reader here that there are two kinds of **solutions** <note>original: *solutiones*</note> (although there are many others, they are ineffective): the one alone is true and natural, while the other is **violent** <note>original: *gewaltsam*; likely referring to the use of strong corrosive acids (like *aqua fortis*) which alchemists believed destroyed the "vital seed" of the metal rather than liberating it.</note>, and under which all the others are categorized. # Of the Preparation of the Stone 39

...the earth. O wonderful Nature, which brings forth miraculous fruits out of the water in the earth, and can give them life from the air. This happens now, and yet the eyes of common people do not see it; but the eyes of understanding and of the spirit perceive it through a true, truthful vision. For the eyes of the **Sages** <term>Sages: the "Wise" or alchemists who understand the hidden workings of the universe.</term> see Nature differently than common eyes. 

<margin>Wonderful operation of Nature.</margin>

As an example: common eyes see that the sun is warm, but the eyes of the Sages see rather that the sun is cold, but its motion <note>original Latin: *motus*</note> is warm. For its work and effect are recognized from the distance of places; the fire of Nature is precisely the same and just so. 

<margin>The sun in itself is cold and not warm, but its motion is warm. Bernard of Trevisan, *On Alchemy*, part 3. <note>original: *Trevis. de Alh. par. 3*</note></margin>

For just as the sun is the center and middle among the planetary spheres <note>literally "planetary circles"</note>, and from this center of the Heavens <note>original Latin: *centro cœli*</note> distributes heat downwards through its motion, so also in the center of the earth is the Sun of the Earth, which through its everlasting movement and motion drives heat or rays upward onto the earth. 

<margin>Correspondence of the heavenly and earthly sun in the center of heaven and earth. <note>original Latin: *in centro cœli & terræ*</note></margin>

This same internal heat is much more powerful than this elemental fire, but it is tempered <note>moderated or balanced</note> with earthly water, which from day to day penetrates through the pores of the earth and cools it. Thus also the air tempers the heavenly sun and its heat, which from day to day runs through the world; and if it were not so, all things would be consumed by such heat, and nothing would grow. Just as now that same invisible...

<margin>The air tempers the heat of the heavenly sun.</margin>

But someone might say: if that were so, it would always happen thus, whereas often no wind at all is noticed.

To this I answer: if water is not poured into a distillation flask <term>Distillation flask: A glass vessel (often called a retort or cucurbit) used in alchemy to heat liquids and capture their vapors.</term> with force, it produces no wind, for a small amount of water creates a small amount of wind. You also see that it does not thunder all the time, even if there are strong winds and rains, but only when, through the force of the Air, the turbid water is driven upward toward the Circle of Fire <note>In the medieval/Renaissance view of the cosmos, the "Circle of Fire" was the highest elemental sphere, located just below the moon.</note>, for Fire tolerates no water.

<margin>Fire and water are directly opposed to one another.</margin>

You can see this before your eyes when you pour cold water into a hot oven, from which something like thunder arises. Why the water does not enter such places and caverns in a uniform way, the reason is this: that there are many such vessels and places. Sometimes a cavern drives forth wind and water from itself for several days or months, until the water is driven back again, as we see in the sea, whose waves are often driven for several miles before they strike something by which they are driven back.

<margin>Heat, a cause of the Air and of Life.</margin>

For the Light of Nature extends that far and reveals what is internal. Regarding this, I give you the following example. <margin>Example</margin> Suppose one dresses a youth of twelve years and a maiden of the same age in the same uniform clothing; when they stand next to each other, no one can recognize which is the boy or the girl, for our eyes cannot penetrate through the fabric. Therefore, our sight deceives us and takes the false appearance for the true reality. But if their clothes are removed and they are stripped bare so that they are seen in the form Nature created them, then each can easily be recognized in their sex <note>original: "Geschlecht"; in this historical context, the author is using biological sex as a metaphor for the distinct "signatures" or essential identities of natural substances.</note>. In the very same way, our understanding casts a shadow upon the shadow of Nature: for the naked body of a person is merely the shadow of the seed of Nature. Just as the human body is covered with clothes, so too is human nature covered by a physical body; the covering or uncovering of which God has reserved for Himself alone. ...a quite beautiful and delightful island / the beautiful island was oriented toward the South <note>original: *gegen Mittag* — literally "toward midday," the standard early modern German term for the South.</note> / and was richly endowed with everything / that man requires for necessity / as well as pleasure: the **Elysian Fields of Virgil** could hardly be compared with it.

For although worms have eyes, sight, hearing, and other external senses, <margin>Worms arise from putrefaction: their mother-vessel is the earth. <note>original: *Vermes ex putredine oriuntur: horum Matrix terra est.* This reflects the pre-modern belief in spontaneous generation, where life was thought to emerge directly from decaying matter.</note></margin> they nonetheless arise from putrefaction, and the place, or earth, in which they rot is their female counterpart. So too in the Philosophical Work, the mother of this thing is your so-often repeated Water; whatever is born from it is born through putrefaction, just like the worms. Therefore the Philosophers have called it a **Phoenix** and a **Salamander** <margin>Phoenix, Salamander.</margin> <note>Both the Phoenix (rising from ashes) and the Salamander (living in fire) are symbols of the Stone's ability to undergo "death" in the crucible and emerge perfected and indestructible.</note>: For if it came from the conception of two bodies, it would be a work subject to death; but because it only makes itself alive again after the destruction of the first body, there shines forth another, indestructible body. <margin>What is death? <note>original: *Mors quid.*</note></margin> Since the death of all things is nothing other than the separation of one thing from another, and so it happens in this Phoenix, that it separates itself with life from the destructible body by its own power."

66 [The Conclusion of] the Author Phil. Räz. to the Children of Truth

<margin>Note: One single thing, mixed with the Philosophical Water. <note>original: *Vna res sola, mixta Aqua philosophica.*</note></margin> I asked further: "My Lord, are there different types of materials, or a complex composition of substances in this work?" But he spoke: "It is only one single thing, with which nothing else is mixed except the **Philosophical Water** <note>The 'Philosophical Water' or 'Mercurial Water' is the transformative solvent used to break down the physical matter (the 'Body') into its spiritual essence.</note> which has been revealed to you so often in your sleep. This water must be ten parts to every one part of the **Body** <note>The 'Body' refers to the solid metallic or mineral base being transformed. A 10:1 ratio of solvent to matter is a specific instruction for the stage of dissolution.</note>. Believe firmly and without doubt, my son, that what has been revealed to you in your sleep (according to the custom of this island) by me and by **Saturn** <note>Saturn, though representing lead and the darkness of the first stage, here serves as a guardian of deep wisdom and the 'pure truth' of the work.</note> is no mere dream, but rather the pure truth. This truth can only be discovered through experience—the sole mistress of all things—with the help of Divine assistance." As I attempted to ask further, he took his leave and awakened me from my sleep without answering,

<margin>Eutopia, that is, a place of blessedness. <note>original Greek: *εὐτόπια* (Eutopia); Latin: *locus beatitudinis*. The author distinguishes 'Eutopia' (the good place) from the more common 'Utopia' (no place).</note></margin> and brought me into the long-desired **Eutopian Realm** (or the Good-Land). Therefore, kind reader, I ask that you let what has been said here be sufficient, and be content with it; fare you well.

To the **Three-in-One** <note>A reference to the Holy Trinity, often mirrored in alchemy by the trinity of Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury.</note> alone be praise, glory, and honor, for all eternity.
Amen.

## On Mercury.

->That is<-

->A Philosophical, Nature-accordant, and Well-founded Dialogue held between **Mercury**, an Alchemist, and **Nature**, in which the properties of the **Philosophical Mercury** <note>In alchemy, 'Philosophical Mercury' is not common quicksilver, but the universal animating spirit or 'solvent' required to transform base metals into gold.</note> are very clearly and brightly revealed.<-

->**Thus make wise things.**<- <note>original: *Also mach weyse ding.* This phrase is a known anagram used by the famous alchemist Michael Sendivogius (Cosmopolita).</note>

<note>A woodcut illustration depicts Mercury (the Greek Hermes) seated in a celestial chariot. He is shown with his traditional attributes: a winged hat and winged sandals, symbolizing his role as the swift messenger between the earthly and divine realms. In his hand, he holds the caduceus, a staff entwined by two serpents representing the union of opposites. The chariot is pulled by a large rooster, a bird that symbolizes the herald of light and the 'awakening' of the matter in the laboratory. Within the wheels of the chariot, small human figures are depicted, representing the various stages or 'labors' of the alchemical process. The scene is set against a backdrop of clouds, emphasizing the volatile, airy nature of Mercury.</note>

68

### A Short Preface to the Dialogue

<note>A decorative initial "W" begins the text.</note> Who the writer of this little book may be is unnecessary to know. However, as to why he has written this, hear the reason. It is also unnecessary that you should know who I am. Yet know specifically, in the highest truth, that the author of this little book knows how to make the **Philosophical Stone**, <note>The 'Philosophical Stone' was the legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and providing the Elixir of Life.</note> has made and prepared it, and still has and possesses it. 

Since we both hold each other in high regard due to a trusted friendship and are well-disposed toward one another, I asked him if he would—as a testimony of our friendship (as the title also implies)—explain the three first primary things, namely **Mercury**, **Sulfur**, and **Salt**, <note>Known as the *Tria Prima*, these three principles were defined by Paracelsus as the fundamental components of all material things: Spirit (Mercury), Soul (Sulfur), and Body (Salt).</note> and provide a short and simple report on whether the **Philosophers' Stone** is to be sought in these or in something else. 

Accordingly, after he had complied with my request in short order with this little treatise, I have entirely <column-break/> <note>This catchword "entirely" [German: *gänzlichen*] indicates the sentence continues on the following page.</note>

<summary>A preface by an anonymous editor asserting that the author of the work is a true adept who possesses the Philosophers' Stone and has written this guide to explain the three primary alchemical principles.</summary>
<keywords>Philosophers' Stone, Mercury, Sulfur, Salt, Tria Prima, alchemy, secret knowledge, Michael Sendivogius</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the editor's preface. The previous page ended with the editor deciding "entirely" [German: *gänzlichen*] to share the author’s work with the public.</meta>

Preface.                                    69

[entirely] held the opinion that if I were to 
communicate the same to others (although 
I would do the writer little or minor favor 
in this, since he is not in the least bit 
boastful) <note>original: "ruhmsichtig"</note> so that it might be promoted 
into public print, the lovers of true 
Wisdom would be well-disposed toward 
me for this and be grateful: because 
henceforth, by reading this little book, 
they will expend less cost, loss of time, 
and loss of honor. <note>Many alchemical seekers spent their entire fortunes and ruined their reputations pursuing false recipes; the editor suggests this book provides a shortcut to the truth.</note> 
When I then perceive or notice that 
pious souls will be grateful (since I hold 
the nonsensical, itinerant alchemists in 
no regard, but blow them to the wind 
and drive them from me), <note>The "itinerant alchemists" (Landläuffige Alchymisten) were traveling charlatans or "puffers" who moved from town to town, often deceiving patrons with chemical tricks to secure funding for "experiments" that never succeeded.</note> 
I will apply myself so that the other 
two remaining treatises by the writer, 
on **Sulfur** and **Salt**, <note>The editor promises that if this work on Mercury is successful, the remaining two parts of the author's trilogy on the *Tria Prima*—the three fundamental principles of matter—will be published.</note> shall also 
follow hereafter. Farewell, and in 
the meantime, make use of this 
little book.

<meta>This page begins the main text of the treatise after the editor's preface. The previous page ended with a catchword signaling the start of this "Dialogue."</meta>

70 Dialogue between Nature and Mercury

# Dialogue and Conversation of
# Nature, Mercury, and an
# Alchemist.

<margin>The occasion of this writing <note>original: "Occasio scripti"</note></margin> Once upon a time, alchemists gathered together and held a general council to discuss how the **Philosophical Stone** <note>The "Philosophical Stone" was the legendary goal of alchemy, believed to be capable of transmuting base metals into gold and acting as an elixir of life.</note> should be prepared. They decided among themselves that each person present should bring their own opinion to light. This assembly took place under the open, free sky, upon a heath, and moreover on a clear day. There, many of them unanimously agreed that Quicksilver or **Mercury** was the **First Matter** <term>First Matter: often called *Prima Materia*, this is the primal, unformed substance that alchemists believed was the starting point for creating the Stone.</term>. Others, however, favored **Sulfur**, while still others proposed something else entirely. Nevertheless, Mercury or Quicksilver was the primary subject of discussion, especially based on the writings of the **Philosophers** <note>In this context, "Philosophers" refers to the ancient and medieval authorities on alchemy, such as Hermes Trismegistus or Geber, whose texts served as the "bible" for later practitioners.</note>, since they identify it as the true Matter, as well as the First Matter of the metals. Since the Philosophers cry out

<margin>Mercury is the first matter of metals and the Stone. <note>original: "Mercurius prima metallorum & lapidis materia."</note></margin> and shout, "Our Mercury, our Quicksilver!" Indeed, while they were thus contending and struggling with one another with great effort (as each was eagerly awaiting the conclusion of this question), a heavy storm occurred in the meantime, with thunder, lightning, and hail, and unheard-of winds and downpours. This turbulence scattered the assembly, driving each person individually into foreign lands and provinces.

<summary>The treatise opens with a gathering of alchemists debating whether Mercury or Sulfur is the "First Matter" of the Philosophical Stone. Their council is violently interrupted and dispersed by a sudden, supernatural storm.</summary>
<keywords>Mercury, Quicksilver, First Matter, Philosophical Stone, Alchemists' Council, Sulfur, Philosophers</keywords>

->and an Alchemist.<- 71

...had struck down, divided, and completely scattered [the assembly], so that the final conclusion was then hindered, postponed, and left unfinished.

Nonetheless, in the time that followed, each individual imagined for themselves what the conclusion of this disputation and conflict might be. Therefore, each one set to work and began—one in this matter, the other in another—to seek out the **Philosophical Stone**, which continues to happen incessantly even to this day. 

One of them in particular, still mindful of the conversation held—namely, that the Stone of the Wise was to be prepared from **Mercury** or Quicksilver—said these words to himself: "Even though no conclusion was reached, I will nonetheless work with Mercury or Quicksilver; yes, I myself will reach the conclusion and prepare this blessed Stone." 

For he was a man who talked and murmured much to himself in secret, as is the habit of alchemists. Therefore, he began to read the books of the **Philosophers** <note>In alchemy, "Philosophers" refers to the ancient authorities whose writings provided the theoretical basis for the Great Work.</note>, and he came across the book of **Alanus** <note>Likely Alanus de Insulis (Alain de Lille), a 12th-century scholar to whom various alchemical treatises were attributed.</note> where he treats Mercury or Quicksilver.

<margin>One must conclude first with the mind before laboring with the hands. <note>original Latin: "Concludendum prius mente, quam manibus laborandum."</note></margin> Thus, this alchemist became a "Philosopher," yet without a final conclusion. Therefore, he took the Quicksilver and began to work; he put it in a glass over the fire. Mercury, as is its nature, smoked away and vanished. 

The wretched alchemist, as he [perceived the nature] of the Quicksilver...

...did not know the nature of **Quicksilver**, he struck his wife and said: "No one else could have come near it but you," therefore he said, "you have taken the **Mercury** out of the glass." The wife howled and excused herself, yet spoke secretly to herself regarding the man: "Nothing but filth <note>The German "Dreck" implies both worthless dirt and excrement, foreshadowing the alchemist's next steps.</note> will come of it for you anyway." The alchemist took more **Mercury**, put it once again into the glass, and guarded it diligently so that the wife should by no means come to it or touch it. But the **Mercury** flew out through the chimney once again. At this, the alchemist reflected that the first matter of the **Stone** must be volatile <note>In alchemical theory, "volatile" substances (those that flee the fire) must be "fixed" so they no longer evaporate.</note>; he rejoiced greatly at this, believing that he could never fail now, and that he had the right matter in his hands. He therefore began to tackle the **Mercury** boldly; he learned further to sublime <term>sublimation: a process where a solid is heated into a gas and then condensed back into solid form, intended to purify the substance</term> it, and to calcine <term>calcination: heating a substance to high temperatures in air to achieve oxidation or reduction to powder</term> it in many ways: now with salt, another time with sulfur, also with blood, with hair, soon with *Aqua Fortis* <note>original Latin: "strong water," referring to nitric acid</note>, again with herbs, urine, vinegar, and the like. But in all of this, he found nothing for his purpose. Moreover, there is nothing in the world with which he did not torment the good **Mercury** or quicksilver. When he had accomplished absolutely nothing, even in the slightest degree, this saying occurred to him: that it is found in the dung.

<margin>The stone is found in the dung. <note>original Latin: "In sterquilinio lapis reperitur." A common alchemical paradox suggesting that the most precious substance is found in the most despised place.</note></margin>

Thereupon he befouled the **Mercury** with all sorts of filth and dung, both together and separately: among these manifold labors, he was [carried] away—

he became tired afterward, and stood by himself in deep, heavy thoughts. Finally, he fell asleep over them. In his sleep, an old man appeared to him, greeted him, and said: "Friend, why do you grieve and trouble yourself?" But he spoke: "I would dearly like to make the **Philosophical Stone**." <note>The "Philosophical Stone" or Philosophers' Stone was the legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and granting eternal life.</note> Upon this, the old man asked: "Friend, from what do you want to make it?" 

Alchemist: From quicksilver or **Mercury**. <note>original: "Mercurio," the Latin dative form of Mercury.</note>

The Old Man: Yes, but from what kind of **Mercury**? 

Alchemist: There is indeed no more than one. 

The Old Man: It is true, nonetheless, that there is only one **Mercury**; however, the same is divided into many kinds, and one part of it is also purer than the other. 

Alchemist: O Sir, I know skill enough to purify it in the very best way with vinegar, saltpeter, and vitriol. <note>Saltpeter (potassium nitrate) and vitriol (sulfuric acid or metal sulfates) were common corrosive agents used by alchemists to "cleanse" metals.</note>

The Old Man: Believe me, that is not the right purification; nor is this the right, true **Mercury**. The ancient Sages and Philosophers have another quicksilver and **Mercury**, <term>Mercury of the Philosophers: a theoretical "prime matter" that alchemists believed was the hidden soul of common mercury, necessary for the Great Work.</term> and another method of purification. 

With that, the old man disappeared. you lack it; you will learn nothing from me if you do not already know it, my Lord Philosopher.
**Alchemist:** What? Do you speak to me in this way, as if to a lowly person? With that, Mercury vanished. The Alchemist cries out after him and calls, but no one would give him an answer. Meanwhile, he thinks to himself and says: "Truly, I am an upright man; Mercury has spoken with me himself; surely he loves me." ->...of Mercury<-                                  80

<margin>If he himself remains ignorant, he is far removed from this art. — Geber <note>original: *ipso ignoraverit, hic multum remotus est ab hac arte. Geber.* This refers to Jabir ibn Hayyan (Geber), the legendary father of alchemy. The note warns that without true understanding of the chemical "fire" or principles, the seeker is lost.</note></margin>

The **Alchemist** <note>original: *Alchymist*</note> continued in his folly, believing that his crude materials would eventually yield the treasure. He spoke of **Mercury** <note>original: *Mercurius*</note> as if he were a common servant, yet he behaved like no true **Philosopher** <note>original: *Philosophus*</note>. Indeed, among the true **Philosophers** <note>original: *Philosophis*</note>, such a man is seen as a "puffer"—one who blows the bellows without any grasp of the spirit. 

<margin>Behold the man! <note>original: *Ecce homo!* A biblical phrase used here with biting irony to mock the Alchemist’s pathetic state and his hubris in claiming to master Nature.</note></margin>

He claimed to follow the path of the ancient **Philosophers** <note>original: *Philosophos*</note>, yet he treated the **Philosopher** <note>original: *Philosophum* <gloss>meaning the "Philosopher’s Stone" or the living spirit of the metal</gloss></note> with utter contempt. "O **Philosopher**!" <note>original: *Philosophe*</note> cried Mercury in a vision, "how can you hope to find light in such darkness?" By persisting in his work with **Mercury** <note>original: *Mercurio*</note> in such a filthy manner, the man only ensured his own poverty and the mockery of the world.

<summary>Mercury mocks the Alchemist for his ignorance and his attempt to use foul substances, while marginal notes from the authority Geber underscore the Alchemist's lack of true knowledge.</summary>
