**Mercury**: You say that I am inconstant, but I refute you in this: I am constant to a steadfast artist, and I am **fixed** to a **fixed** master. <note>In alchemical terminology, "fixity" refers to a substance's ability to remain stable under the heat of fire without evaporating. Mercury is traditionally the "volatile" spirit that must be "fixed" to create the Stone.</note> But you and your kind are changeable and inconstant: you jump from one thing to another, from one **matter** to the next.

**Alchemist**: Then tell me this: are you that same **Mercury** of whom the **Philosophers** <note>original: *Philosophi*</note> wrote, saying that together with **Sulfur** and **Salt** <note>These are the *Tria Prima*, the three prime substances (Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury) that Paracelsian alchemy claimed were the components of all material things.</note> he is the beginning of all things, or must one seek another?

**Mercury**: It is true; the fruit does not fall far from the tree, yet I in no way seek my own glory: I am exactly the same as I was before. But my age and my years are different.

**Alchemist**: Now you please me, because you say that you are somewhat old; for I have always sought after such a one who is more mature and more **fixed**, so that I might more easily come to an end <note>The "end" refers to the successful completion of the Great Work, the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note> with him.

**Mercury**: You seek me in vain and to no purpose in my old age, you who did not know me in my youth.

**Alchemist**: How? Should I not have known you, when I have worked with you in many ways at all times, as you yourself have witnessed, and I will not cease until I have obtained the **Philosophers' Stone**? <note>original: *Philosophen Stain*</note>

**Mercury**: Oh, poor me! What shall I begin to do? I fear that I must perhaps be stained and defiled again with coal and dung. Thus has...

# 16 Dialogue on the Nature of Mercury

**Alchemist:** I do not understand what you say, much less the fire of which you speak. **Mercury:** He who knows the fire of my heart sees that the fire (namely, the proper warmth) <note>original: *die gebührende wärmb*. This refers to the alchemical concept of *calor naturae*, a gentle, regulated heat similar to a brooding hen, rather than a destructive furnace fire.</note> is my food and nourishment; and the more the spirit of my heart is fed with fire, the more fruitful and fat it becomes. The death and passing away of this spirit is thereafter the life of all things that are to be found in this, my kingdom.

**Alchemist:** Are you great or powerful? **Mercury:** Consider me as an example: out of 1000 droplets I become one thing; from a single one I divide myself into many 1000 droplets. Even as you have me bodily before your eyes and know how to play with me <note>A reference to the physical properties of liquid quicksilver, which breaks into tiny beads and effortlessly recombines into a single mass.</note>, you can divide me into as many pieces as you please, and yet I will become one thing again. What then shall my spirit (the inner heart) accomplish? Which at all times brings forth uncountably many thousands from the very smallest part.

**Alchemist:** How should I then conduct myself with you, so that I might prepare you in this way? **Mercury:** Inwardly I am a fire; fire is my food. But the life of the fire is the air; without air the fire is extinguished <note>The alchemist notes that fire requires "pneuma" or air to breathe, just as the "spirit" of the metal requires a medium to work within.</note>. The fire surpasses the air. Therefore, I have no rest nor peace, and no common air can chain or keep me. Add air to air, so that they both become one and are weighty or heavy.

**Alchemist:** What will ultimately come of it? **Mercury:** The superflu-

<margin>The sum of the whole work <note>original: *Summa totius ope[ris]*</note></margin>

fige <note>This is a catchword, likely leading to the German word *vberflüßige* (superfluous or abundant) on the following page.</note>

<summary>Mercury explains that his essence is sustained by a specific "proper warmth" and describes his ability to multiply and recombine, emphasizing the role of air in sustaining his internal fire.</summary>
<keywords>Mercury, quicksilver, internal fire, proper warmth, multiplication, air, spirit of the heart, alchemy of droplets</keywords>

**Dialogue of Nature and Mercury**

**Nature:** Know that I have only one such son—only one, I say, one out of seven <note>Referring to the seven metals of antiquity, each associated with a celestial body; Mercury is the primary "son" or root of them all.</note>—who is the very first, and who is also all in all. He is everything, I say, who was once a single thing and yet is nothing; thereafter, his number is perfect and whole. Within him are the four elements, yet he himself is no element. He is a spirit, and yet has no body. He is a man and yet takes the place of a woman; or rather, he is of both male and female nature—that is, a hermaphrodite or a hybrid child <term>hermaphrodite: in alchemy, the Rebis or "double thing," representing the union of opposites (sulfur and mercury, male and female)</term>. He is a boy and yet carries manly weapons; he is a beast, and yet has wings like a bird. He is a poison and yet heals leprosy; he is life, and yet kills all things. He is a king, yet another possesses his kingdom. He flies away together with the fire. He is a water, and yet does not wet <note>original: *Waſſer/ vnd netzet doch nicht*. This is a classic alchemical riddle for Mercury, which is liquid at room temperature but does not soak into surfaces like water.</note>. He is an earth, and yet is sown. He is an air, and yet lives in the water.

**Alchemist:** Now I see that I know nothing; but I dare not say so, for I would lose my reputation and praise, and none of my friends would hold me in any regard anymore. Yet I will speak and act as if I knew much, otherwise no one would give me so much as a piece of bread; for there are many who hope for great wealth from me.

**Nature:** But what if you carry on this way for a long time? What kind of end will it reach? In the end, every one of your friends will want their own property back.

**Alchemist:** I will feed them all with good hope: As long as [Catchword]

It addresses the reader directly, justifying the author's cryptic style and explaining his motivations for writing, despite his desire for anonymity and his previous intent to save this material for a larger volume.</meta>

93

**Preface / of the Author to**
**the Reader.**

<margin>A modest apology from the author.</margin> <note>original Latin: "Excusatio modesta authoris."</note>
**S**ince it is not fitting for me, <note>The original text begins with a decorated initial letter **D**, for the German word *Dieweilen*, meaning "since" or "because."</note> Gracious Reader, to describe these matters more clearly than other ancient Philosophers have done, you will perhaps not be well satisfied with my writings. Especially since you have so many other Philosophical books at hand; but believe me, it is also not necessary for me to write books, as I seek neither profit nor vain glory from them; for which reason I also do not desire to broadcast who I am. What I have already allowed to come to the light of day <note>An idiom for "to publish."</note> for your benefit, I thought would be more than enough; and I intended to save the rest for the *Harmony*, <note>This refers to the *Harmonia*, or *The Harmony of the Three Kingdoms of Nature*, a work by Michael Sendivogius that aims to explain the universal laws of alchemy and nature through the animal, vegetable, and mineral realms.</note> where I have discussed natural things at length. However, at the urging of good friends, I have also intended and been obliged to write this treatise on sulfur, in which whether some-
                                        thing

<summary>In this preface, the author justifies his cryptic writing style by appealing to the tradition of ancient philosophers and expresses his indifference toward fame or profit. He explains that he was persuaded by friends to publish this specific treatise on sulfur rather than waiting for his larger work, the Harmony.</summary>
<keywords>Preface, Michael Sendivogius, Sulfur, Harmony, Alchemical Philosophy, Anonymity, Authorship</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's preface to his treatise on sulfur. The text flows from the previous page’s discussion regarding the author’s reluctance to publish more, continuing the sentence: "whether something was necessary to add to the previous..."</meta>

94                                        ->Preface.<-

...thing <note>This word completes the sentence from the previous page: "...whether something was necessary to add..."</note> was necessary to add to the previous, I cannot know. Indeed, this treatise will also not be quite to your liking if so many books of the philosophers are not enough for you already. Primarily, however, if the daily workings of Nature are not a sufficient example for you, then no examples or illustrations will be of much help to you. For if you weigh such things—just as Nature performs them—with mature judgment, you would not need so many books of the Sages; since, as it seems to me, it is much better to learn from the Master herself—Nature—than from the students. You have understood well enough in the Preface to the *Twelve Treatises*, <note>This refers to the *Novum Lumen Chymicum* (New Chemical Light), a famous alchemical work by Michael Sendivogius consisting of twelve treatises on the principles of nature.</note> as well as in the first treatise of the same, that so many and such large books are found in this art <term>this art: Alchemy, often referred to simply as "The Art" or the "Hermetic Art."</term> that they now hinder those who seek much more than they help. And it also appears to be so, because all the writings of the philosophers have grown from such a small tablet of Hermes <note>A reference to the *Tabula Smaragdina* or "Emerald Tablet," a very short, foundational text of alchemy attributed to Hermes Trismegistus.</note> into such a large, sprawling labyrinth, and daily they incline more toward obscurity. I believe, however, that it was done by those who know only out of 
                                        begrudging envy <note>The German *mißgunst* implies a malicious withholding of knowledge or ill-will; the author suggests the complexity of alchemical literature is a deliberate attempt to hide the truth from the unworthy.</note>

<summary>The author argues that observing the natural world is a better teacher than reading the vast and often confusing library of alchemical texts. He laments that the simple truths of the Emerald Tablet have been obscured by a labyrinth of complex writings, which he attributes to the envy of previous masters.</summary>
<keywords>Nature as Master, Emerald Tablet, Hermes Trismegistus, Twelve Treatises, alchemical labyrinth, Sendivogius, secrecy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's preface, specifically addressing how alchemical texts become corrupted through the ignorance or malice of scribes. It completes the sentence from the previous page: "I believe, however, that it was done by those who know only out of..."</meta>

**Preface. 95**

...malice <note>This completes the thought from the previous page: "...I believe, however, that it was done by those who know only out of malice."</note>, for an ignorant person does not understand what he should remove from or add to a text; unless, perhaps, the author’s original manuscript was illegible. If in any art <term>art: alchemy, often called the "Hermetic Art"</term> it matters greatly whether there is one word too many or too few, it is certainly in this one. <margin>In this respect, Alchemy is equal to Theology <note>original Latin: *Hac in parte Chimia Theologiæ par est*. This refers to the idea that in both alchemy and scripture, the addition or subtraction of a single word can lead to heresy or total failure.</note></margin>

To give an example: it is written in one place, "afterward, mix these waters with one another." Someone then added the word (not). Although he added very little, he nevertheless reversed the meaning of the entire chapter. Yet every diligent student should note that a bee <note>original: *Imme*</note> collects honey even from poisonous herbs; for if he conducts his work according to the possibilities of Nature, he will be able to separate the false from the true without difficulty. One should not, however, stop reading, for one book explains another.

I understand it thus: that the philosophical books of Geber <margin>Geber, Alchemist, King of the Arabs <note>original: *Geberus Alchimista Rex Arabum*. Geber is the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, a 8th-century polymath and the most influential figure in early alchemy.</note></margin> (and God knows if this is not true of other Sages as well) are enchanted with much "poison," <note>In alchemical literature, "poison" often refers to "blinds" or intentional obscurities meant to protect secrets from the uninitiated.</note> in such a way...

<summary>The author explains how even a single-word change, such as adding "not," can ruin an alchemical recipe, comparing the precision of the craft to theology. He advises students to act like bees—extracting truth from even "poisonous" or corrupted texts by comparing different books and following the laws of Nature.</summary>
<keywords>textual corruption, Geber (Jabir ibn Hayyan), Alchemy and Theology, Nature as teacher, alchemical blinds, Hermetic Art</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's preface regarding the intentional obscurity of alchemical writings, specifically referencing the works of Geber (Jabir ibn Hayyan) mentioned at the end of page 95.</meta>

->96 Preface.<-

...[poisoned], <note>Continuing the sentence from page 95 regarding Geber’s "poisoned" or intentionally corrupted texts.</note> so that they can or should in no way be understood, even if one were to read them a thousand times—and that by an intelligent reader. (Fools shall be entirely turned away and excluded from his books.) <margin>Just like Lord Andreas. O. E. <note>original Latin: *Sicut Dominus Andreas*. This likely refers to a contemporary figure or a specific scholar known to the author's circle.</note></margin> Many are indeed found who interpret his works, as well as those of other authors; but I always find the interpretation more difficult than the text itself.

My advice is that you stick to the text, and whatever you read, judge it all according to the possibilities of Nature; and above all things, investigate what Nature is. They all write, indeed, that it <note>The "it" here refers to the *Materia Prima* or the starting substance of the Great Work.</note> is a simple, easy, and common thing; and while that is true, they should have added: "for the one who knows." 

He who knows it recognizes it even under the dung; he who does not know it does not even believe that it exists within gold. And all those who have written such obscure books—if they did not already know the Art <term>the Art: alchemy, specifically the process of transmutation</term>, but instead had to seek it for the first time from such books (which are indeed truthful), it would be [difficult] for them... <note>The sentence continues onto the next page.</note>

<summary>The author cautions that alchemical texts are intentionally designed to baffle the "foolish" and that commentaries often confuse more than they clarify. He advises students to study Nature directly and notes that the secret of alchemy is hidden in plain, common things that the ignorant overlook.</summary>
<keywords>Geber, hermetic intentional obscurity, laws of Nature, interpretation, Materia Prima, the Art of Alchemy</keywords>

--- Page 123 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument that if the ancient masters had not already known the Art, they would have found it harder to learn from their own books than modern seekers do. It transitions into the author's justification for his own writing style.</meta>

->Preface. 97<-

...it would be far more difficult for them to discover than for the researchers of today. I do not wish to boast of my own books; let them be judged by someone who evaluates them according to the possibilities and the course of Nature. 

But whoever cannot understand from my writings, counsels, and examples the workings of Nature and her servants—those living spirits <note>In alchemical thought, "spirits" usually refer to volatile substances or the vital forces that animate material bodies.</note> that harden the air, and which are the very subject and name of the First Matter <term>First Matter: Also called *Materia Prima*, this is the primordial, formless substance that alchemists believe is the starting point for creating the Philosopher's Stone.</term>—he will hardly be able to understand them from the works of Ramon Llull <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315) was a philosopher and logician; a large body of incredibly complex and coded alchemical literature was attributed to him, which was famous for being nearly impossible to decipher.</note>. For it is scarcely possible to believe that the spirits possess such power "in the belly of the wind" <note>This phrase refers to the *Tabula Smaragdina* (Emerald Tablet), one of the most famous foundational texts of alchemy, which claims the "wind carried it in its belly," signifying the spiritual or gaseous state of the Stone's components.</note>. 

I too have had to wander through this forest <note>The "forest" or "thicket" is a standard alchemical metaphor for the confusing, dark, and dangerous path of study and the obfuscated language of previous masters.</note> and have even helped to increase its growth; yet I have done so in such a way that my "plants" shall serve as signs, and as it were, lanterns and guides for the true children of the Art and researchers of this science who wish to travel through these thickets. For my little plants are, so to speak, corporeal spirits; for the times are long gone when true trust flourished among good friends, and one [helped] the other...

<summary>The author presents his writings as a guiding light through the metaphorical "forest" of alchemical confusion, claiming his explanations of the "First Matter" are more accessible than those of Ramon Llull. He laments the decline of the era when alchemists shared their secrets openly among friends.</summary>
<keywords>Ramon Llull, First Matter, spirits, Emerald Tablet, forest of alchemy, children of the Art, corporeal spirits</keywords>

...taught others this art by word of mouth; today, however, it is obtained only through the inspiration of the Most High. For this reason, no diligent and God-fearing seeker should despair. If he seeks it rightly, he will find it, for it can be obtained more easily from God than from men. He is a God of inexpressible mercy who cannot abandon the faithful; with Him, there is no respect of persons <note>original: "kein ansehen der Person." This is a biblical sentiment (Romans 2:11) meaning God does not show favoritism based on worldly status or wealth.</note>. A broken and humble heart He will not cast away. He has also had mercy on me, His most unworthy creature. I am far, far too lowly to recount, boast of, or praise His power, goodness, and innumerable mercy, which He has deigned to let me know. Since I can thank Him in no other way, I will not neglect to at least leave this record behind for posterity with my pen. Therefore, be comforted, honor-loving reader; He will not deny you such grace either, if you place all your trust in Him, worship Him, and call upon Him. He will [open] the door of Nature <term>Door of Nature: A common alchemical metaphor for the moment of revelation where the practitioner finally understands the secret laws governing material transformation.</term> to you...

of metals / according to the opinion of the ancient philosophers / [there are] two: Sulfur / and Mercury: but according to the younger ones <note>The "younger ones" refers to the Paracelsian school of alchemists who expanded the traditional two-principle system of Geber (Sulfur and Mercury) to include a third: Salt. We wish to return to our subject / and first treat of the nearest element, the Earth.

### **Of the Element of Earth.**

The Earth is an element / very worthy in its quality / and glory. In this element rest the remaining three / but especially the Fire. It is the foremost element for hiding / and for revealing / what is entrusted to it: it is dense / and porous / heavy / because of its compactness / yet light / on account of its nature: it is the <margin>Earth is the center <note>original: "Terra est centrum."</note></margin> center of the world / and also of the other elements.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the world’s natural mechanics, moving from the four elements and the "Chaos" mentioned on page 111 to the specific function of the Earth's magnetic and geographic poles in the circulation of water.</meta>

112

...therefore, we must consider the governance of this great structure. This world is directed and held in place by its **Poles** <note>original: "Polo"</note>. 

<margin>There are two poles of the world, the arctic and the antarctic.</margin>

For you must understand that there are two **Poles** <note>original: "Poli"</note> of the universe: the **Arctic** <note>original: "Arcticus"</note> in the north, and the **Antarctic** <note>original: "Antarcticus"</note> in the south. These serve as the fixed points upon which the heavens and the spheres of the elements revolve, much like a wheel turns upon its axle.

This movement is essential for the life of the world, for it drives the waters through their courses. Every **Pole** <note>original: "Polus"</note> acts as a point of attraction or expulsion for the great oceans. It is a mystery of nature that these waters are not stagnant; rather, the two **Poles** <note>original: "Poli"</note> facilitate a perpetual flow. 

<margin>Through the pores of the earth.</margin>

This circulation is achieved because the water does not merely reside on the surface of the globe. Instead, it penetrates deep into the interior, moving **through the pores of the earth** <note>Early modern scientists often compared the Earth to a living organism; the "pores" are the subterranean channels and veins that allow water and vapors to circulate through the "body" of the world.</note>. From the **Arctic Pole** <note>original: "Polo Arctico"</note>, the waters are drawn down into the depths, where they are acted upon by the central heat. 

Once processed in the subterranean laboratory of nature, the water is driven out again. Through this constant rotation from one **Pole** <note>original: "Polo"</note> to the other, the Earth is refreshed, and the minerals within its "veins" are nourished by the spirit of the world carried within the water.

<summary>This page explains the role of the Arctic and Antarctic poles in driving the circulation of water through the Earth's interior via subterranean "pores." It characterizes the Earth as a living system where the poles act as the primary engines for recycling the elements.</summary>
<keywords>Poles, Arctic, Antarctic, water circulation, pores of the earth, cosmology, Earth's interior</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the explanation of the global water cycle, specifically refuting the idea that water is created anew (generated) in the atmosphere or by the stars. It argues instead for a subterranean circulation driven by the sea.</meta>

->of Mineral Things. 113<-

...only impress a power and spiritual **influence** <term>influence: <note>original: "Influenz." In early modern physics and alchemy, this referred to an invisible flow of power or "emanation" from celestial bodies or spiritual forces that affected the physical world without having physical mass or weight.</note></term>, which has no weight or heaviness. )

Therefore, the waters are not **generated** <note>In this context, "generated" means created anew out of nothing or transformed from other elements; the author argues the total amount of water is constant and merely circulates.</note>; rather, they are spread throughout the entire world from the middle of the sea through the secret passages of the Earth. From these natural foundations, the **philosophers** <note>Natural philosophers, the predecessors of modern scientists who studied the laws of the physical world.</note> have invented various instruments, water-conduits, and fountain-works, since it is known that water naturally rises no higher than its source. And if it were not so by Nature, **Art** <note>original: "kunſt." In the 16th and 17th centuries, "Art" referred to human technology, engineering, or alchemy.</note> would never be able to achieve it, for **Art follows Nature**, and what is not in Nature cannot be obtained through Art. Thus, as we have said before, water is never driven higher than the place from which it was taken.

Take as an example a **wine-siphon** <note>original: "Weinheber" or "Weinſtäler." A tool used by vintners to draw wine out of a barrel using atmospheric pressure and gravity.</note>, with which one draws wine out of a cask.

Therefore, you should finally know that no water-springs or rivers are generated from the stars, but rather from the middle of the sea, back to which they all flow again, thus maintaining a constant, everlasting cycle. For if that were not the case, nothing at all would be generated either in or upon the Earth; indeed, the destruction of the world would have to follow. 

However, so that no one may object that all water in the sea is salty while all springs are sweet, one should know the cause: that the springs are **distilled** through the passages of the Earth, and for many miles they struggle through narrow places and much sand—

<summary>This page explains the subterranean water cycle, arguing that all freshwater springs originate from the sea and are purified through natural filtration and "distillation" in the earth's crust. It highlights the early modern scientific principle that technology (Art) is limited by and must mimic the laws of Nature.</summary>
<keywords>hydrogeology, distillation, Art vs Nature, wine-siphon, water cycle, natural philosophy, influence</keywords>

114 [space] **On Sulfur / the Second Principle**

...passing through <note>This sentence continues from the previous page, describing how seawater filters through the earth.</note>, and thus losing their salty nature, they become **sweet** <note>In this context, "sweet" (süß) means fresh or potable water, as opposed to "salt" water.</note>, following the example of which cisterns were invented. Yet, in certain places, the passages and fissures are larger and wider, through which salty water also penetrates; from this, salt-springs and brine waters arise, as seen in Hall in Swabia, and Saxony, and other places in Germany. In some places, it is boiled away by heat, so that the salt remains lying in the sand and ground, while the water runs off through other paths, as in Poland at Wiślica and Bochnia. Likewise, when the waters run through hot, sulfurous, and constantly burning places, <margin>The origin of thermal baths <note>original: "Origo thermarū."</note></margin> they become hot, and warm baths spring forth from them; for deep within the Earth there are places where Nature melts, distills, and separates a sulfurous ore when it is ignited by the **central fire** <note>The "Central Fire" (Centralische Fewr) was a common belief in early geology and alchemy, suggesting a heat source at the Earth's core that powered volcanic and geothermal activity.</note>. When the water then runs through such burning places, it is heated by them, depending on whether it flows near or far from them, and thus it breaks forth into the light of day, retaining its sulfurous taste, just as a cooked broth tastes of meat. The same occurs when the water runs through mineral places, such as copper or alum; it takes on a taste from them. Such a **distiller** is the Creator of all things, in whose hand is this **flask** <term>flask: <note>original: "Kolben." A round-bottomed glass vessel used in alchemical heating and distillation.</note></term> and **alembic** <term>alembic: <note>original: "Alembis." A distilling apparatus consisting of two vessels connected by a tube, used to purify substances by evaporation and condensation.</note></term>; according to this example, all distillations were invented by the **philosophers** <note>Natural philosophers or alchemists.</note>—which the Almighty and Merciful God Himself...

# 122 On Sulfur, the Second Principle

<margin>Flint <note>original: "SILeX." In alchemical thought, flint is the perfect example of "hidden fire." The fire is present within the cold stone but remains invisible and unfelt until it is struck (movement).</note></margin> is neither felt nor seen, unless it be awakened and ignited through movement, so that it appears: in the same way is the Fire, in which the Holy Majesty of our Creator resides, motionless, unless it is then awakened and guided by the very will of the Most High, wherever His holy will desires. For through the will of the highest Creator occurs the most intense and most fearsome impulse.

Let one consider a comparison: when a potentate of this world sits in his pomp and glory, dear reader, what a peace and what a silence is all around him? And even if perhaps one of his servants stirs, it is yet only a single movement of one or the other, which no one notices. But when the Lord himself moves, there is a general commotion and uprising, and the entire entourage moves with him.

How much more then, when the Supreme Monarch, the King of all Kings, the Creator of all things (after whose example the princes of this world are modeled), moves His authority—God help us! What a movement that is, what a trembling and quaking, when the entire assembly of the Heavenly Hosts moves around Him.

But someone might ask from where we know these things, since human understanding cannot fathom heavenly matters. To those we answer that these things are known to all philosophers; indeed, the incomprehensible Wisdom has breathed it into them, so that everything [is made] according to the image

80. **Of Sulfur, the Second Principle** <term>Sulfur (Schwefel), alongside Salt and Mercury, was one of the three "Primes" (*Tria Prima*) of alchemy. It represents the soul, the principle of combustion, and the active, solar force in both minerals and humans.</term>

However, after man, through the sin of disobedience, transgressed the command of the Most High, he was cast out into this perishable elemental world—which God had created for the wild beasts alone—to join them. Since he could not live without nourishment, it was necessary for him to seek his sustenance from the corrupted elements of this world. Through this, those pure elements of his original creation became infected and were gradually brought to ruin and decay, until one quality overcame the others, leading to the corruption of the whole frame, to weakness, and finally to separation and death.

Subsequently, those who were born outside of Paradise in these perishable elements, from a corrupted seed, have come much closer to this decay and death. This is because seed grown from perishable food cannot be long-lasting. The further humans are removed from the expulsion from Paradise, the closer they come to perishability; hence the shortening of human life. ### Of Mineral Things. [page number] 731

...easily through a disorderly life and over-indulgence toward destruction. Experience teaches us this, for children conceived by unhealthy parents do not live long. 

However, if man had remained in Paradise—a place suited to his nature where the elements still stood unadulterated in their **virginity** <note>In alchemy, "virginity" refers to the primordial, uncorrupted state of matter before it was "defiled" by the imperfections of the fallen world.</note>—he would have remained immortal for all eternity. For it is certain that when the pure elements are united in an equality of their powers, that very thing is indestructible; and this is exactly how the **Philosophers' Stone** <term>The Philosophers' Stone (Lapis Philosophorum) is the ultimate goal of alchemy, representing a perfectly balanced substance that can transmute metals and heal the human body by restoring its elemental harmony.</term> is meant to be. 

The ancient sages compared the Stone to this original state of human creation. However, the "philosophers" of today—who understand everything only by the literal letter—have mistakenly applied this to the perishable way things are produced in our current time. 

This immortality was the primary reason why the Sages exerted themselves to seek this Stone. For they knew that man had been created from such perfect elements; they meditated, therefore, on this act of creation. And when they discovered that this state was natural <note>The author argues that immortality through elemental balance is a "natural" law of the universe, even if it was lost to humanity after the Fall.</note>, they began to investigate whether one could still obtain such indestructible elements, or if they could be united or introduced into any particular thing. 

The Most High Creator of all things breathed into their minds the realization that such a composition of elements is to be found in **Gold**; for in animals it is impossible, as they are composed of 

K 5 <margin>perishable...</margin>

<summary>The author explains that the Philosophers' Stone mirrors the perfect, immortal elemental balance humans possessed in Paradise. He concludes that while living creatures are now too perishable to hold this balance, it can be found within the "composition" of Gold.</summary>

**ciples of All Things.**

[Decorative initial N] After the description of the 4 Elements, we proceed to the three principles <note>Original: "dreyen anfängen." In alchemical literature, these are the *Tria Prima*: Sulfur, Salt, and Mercury, the fundamental components of all material substance.</note> of all things, which arise from the Elements in the following manner. 

After Nature received the fiefdom <note>Original: "Lehenrecht," referring to feudal land rights granted by a sovereign; here it implies Nature acts as God's steward on Earth.</note> over the Monarchy of this world from God the Almighty Creator of all things, she began to distribute the places and lands to each according to its dignity and worth; and before all things, she appointed the 4 Elements as the Princes of this world. And so that the will of the Most High might be fulfilled (according to whose will Nature must act), she ordained that each should work incessantly upon the other. 

Therefore, Fire began to act within the Air, <margin>🜍 Sulfur</margin> and brought forth **Sulfur**. The Air acted upon the Water, <margin>🜔 Salt</margin> and brought forth **Salt**; the Water, however, [acted] upon the Earth, <margin>☿ Mercury</margin> and generated **Mercury**. 

But because the Earth had nothing into which it could act, it brought forth nothing itself; rather, that which was produced [by the others] remained within it. Therefore, only three principles have grown; the Earth, however, has become their Mother and Wet-nurse. These three principles, as mentioned, have grown, which the ancient Philosophers did not take so strictly, recognizing only two effects of the Elements

140 [tab] On the three Prin-
ciples, **Fire** is enough so that it has nourishment; and the external **Fire** is its nourishment, and is to be considered as wood is to the **elemental Fire**; and according to such nourishment, it grows and multiplies itself. [of] all things. 143

white, until [it reaches] **crystalline clarity**. Common **Mercury** is precipitated <note>original: *præcipitirt*. This refers to the chemical process where a solid substance is separated from a solution during a reaction.</note> into a yellow powder and a "bad" sulfur; however, our **Quicksilver** is, by means of heat, converted into a snow-white, good, **fixed** <note>original: *fixen*. From these circumstances, you may now easily observe how far common **Mercury** is distinguished from the **Quicksilver of the Wise**. If you do not yet understand it, then do not wait; no living person will tell it to you more clearly than we have spoken.

(But concerning its **Virtues** <note>original: *Tugenden*. This refers to the inherent powers, medicinal properties, or alchemical "strength" of the substance.</note>.) Our **Quicksilver** is of such virtue that it is sufficient in itself, and [provides] as much for you as for it...

<summary>The author distinguishes philosophical mercury from common mercury by their reactions to heat: common mercury becomes more fluid when boiled, while philosophical mercury thickens and becomes "fixed."</summary>

<keywords>Philosophical Mercury, Quicksilver of the Wise, precipitation, fixation, Sulfur, alchemical boiling, virtues of mercury</keywords>

Yet without knowledge of natural things, especially in the **mineral kingdom**, you will be like a blind man who finds his way only by habit. Such a person seeks the art by chance, and if by luck he occasionally happens upon the right **Matter** of **our Quicksilver**, he stops where he should begin, and thus loses by chance what he found by chance, for he does not know upon what he should ground his reasoning. 

Indeed, this science is a gift from the **Most High** <note>Referring to God.</note>, and unless God reveals it to someone through high understanding or a good friend, it is recognized with great difficulty. ...nothing fresh or new, for they do not follow the possibility of Nature, but rather understand the philosophical writings according to the **letter**. All of these, however, are of that general assembly and **council** <note>This refers to the "Imperial Diet" or assembly of alchemists described in alchemical allegories.</note> about which it is written in the *Dialogue between Mercury and the Alchemist*, who return home again without a resolution: for they seek the end not only without the means, but also without the beginning. ### Of Sulfur

Among the three beginnings, the philosophers have rightly placed **Sulfur** <note>In the alchemical "Tria Prima," Sulfur represents the soul, the active solar principle, and the power of combustion and growth.</note> at the forefront as the most precious beginning; within its preparation, the entire art is contained. For there are three kinds of **Sulfur** <margin>Threefold sulfur: 1. Tincturing or coloring; 2. Coagulating Mercury.</margin> which are primarily to be chosen: a tincturing and coloring sulfur; a sulfur that congeals mercury; and the third, which ripens the essence. Concerning these we should indeed treat and deal with earnestly; however, since we have already [discussed] the one beginning in a conversational manner...

(He was otherwise not of the deceitful sort <note>A "deceitful" alchemist was a "puffer" or con artist; our protagonist is portrayed here as a sincere but misguided seeker.</note>, but was only harmful to himself.) And in the midst of his lamenting, he began to curse **Sulfur**, because it had consumed so much of his money, so much effort, and so much time for nothing.

Now, **Sulfur** was also in that same forest, though without the Alchemist's knowledge. While the Alchemist was thus behaving so miserably, he heard a voice, like that of an old man, saying: "Good friend, why do you complain? Why do you curse **Sulfur**?" 

The Alchemist peered all around him, and since he saw no one, he was startled. But the voice spoke once more: "Good friend, why are you so sad?"

The Alchemist took heart and replied: "Sir, just as a hungry man always thinks of bread, so I think constantly of the **Philosophers' Stone**."

**The Voice:** "And why do you curse **Sulfur**?"

**Alchemist:** "Sir, I believed it to be the **First Matter** <note>original: "erſte matery" or Prima Materia, the primitive, formless base of all matter required to start the Alchemical Work.</note> of the Philosophers' Stone; therefore I have spent many years working with it and have spent much, yet I have not been able to find the **Stone**."

**The Voice:** "Friend, I indeed know the true **Sulfur**, and the most noble...

[Header] of all things. [Page number] 155

...most noble piece of the **Philosophers' Stone** <note>original: "nembste stuck deß Philosophischen Steins." This completes the thought from the previous page that Sulfur is the most vital component of the Stone.</note>; but I do not know you, and I also know nothing of your work or intentions. You curse **Sulfur** quite unjustly, for he lies in a very hard prison and cannot be with everyone, because his feet are shackled, and he is stuck in a dark dungeon, nor does he go out except to where his keepers carry him.

**Alchemist.** And why does he lie imprisoned?

**The Voice.** Because he wanted to comply with all alchemists and do what they desired, against the will of his mother <note>In alchemical allegory, "Mother Nature" often restricts the use of her secrets to protect them from the "puffer" or the unworthy chemist.</note>, who had forbidden him to obey anyone but those she knew well. Therefore, she had him thrown into the dungeon and ordered his feet to be fettered, and she set guards, without whose knowledge and will he can go nowhere.

**Alchemist.** O poor wretch! That is why he could not come to my aid. Surely his mother does him a great injustice. And when will he be released from prison?

**The Voice.** Dear friend, the **Philosophic Sulfur** cannot come out from there except with a very great amount of time and very difficult labor.

**Alchemist.** Sir, who are his guards? Who watches over him?

**The Voice.** Friend, his guards are of his own kind <note>In alchemy, this refers to the metallic "crust" or impurities that are chemically related to the core principle but prevent it from being purified.</note>, but they are tyrannical.

**Alchemist.** Sir, who are you, and what is your name?

**The Voice.** I am the judge and overseer of the prison, and my name is **Saturn** <note>Saturn is the alchemical name for Lead. In this context, he represents the "Nigredo" or blackening stage—a state of darkness, heaviness, and restriction that occurs at the beginning of the transformation.</note>.

**Alchemist.** So **Sulfur** lies imprisoned with you?

**The Voice.** **Sulfur** does indeed lie in my—

**Stiffelius:** Have you not heard that a great flame of fire consumes a small one?

Little ovens, of which the Alchemist had many, were brought into use. But at the conclusion of every work, they only produced little candles <note>The author is mocking the Alchemist; instead of gold, his failed experiments produce only "Kerßlin" (little wax tapers or fire-starters), which were cheap, common items sold by street vendors.</note>, which old women commonly sell for lighting fires. Then Saturn said: "Then watch and learn."

He took two Mercuries of different substance but of one root, which Saturn had washed with his urine <note>In alchemical texts, "urine" (harm) is often a coded term for a specific solvent or reagent, such as an ammoniacal solution or "the water of nature," rather than literal waste.</note> and called the "Sulfur of Sulfurs." <margin>He called those the sulfurs of sulfurs. <note>original: "Vocavit illa sulphura de sulphuribus."</note></margin> He then mixed the fixed with the volatile. Afterward, he placed it in a bath of very gentle heat (as the matter required) and performed everything correctly.

Thus they made the **Stone of the Wise**, for from the right matter follows a right work. 172

# Concluding Speech.

<margin>Every end is terminated above its own beginning. <note>original: "Omnis finis supra suū principium terminatur." This philosophical maxim suggests that the final result of a process is inherently linked to and fulfills its starting point.</note></margin>

Every seeker of this Art should, above all things, examine with mature consideration the creation, action, and powers of the four elements within his process. For if he does not know this origin and Nature, he will not come to the knowledge of the "beginnings" <note>original: "Anfäng." In this context, it refers to the Three Principles (Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury) which serve as the bridge between the raw elements and physical matter.</note>, nor will he ever recognize the true matter of the Stone; much less will he achieve a good end, because every end rises out of its beginning. He who well knows what he begins, also well knows what he will end. For the origin of the elements is a Chaos <note>In alchemy, Chaos refers to the "Prima Materia" or the disorganized, primal state of matter before it is shaped by divine or natural forces.</note>, out of which God, the Creator of all things, created and separated the elements, which is the work of God alone.

Out of the elements, however, Nature brings forth the beginnings of things; and that belongs, according to the will of God, to Nature alone. Subsequently, out of these beginnings, Nature makes minerals, ores, and all things. From these, the Artist <note>The "Artist" refers to the alchemist who practices the "Art."</note>, in imitation of Nature, is capable of many wonderful things, because Nature brings forth the ores, metals, and all manner of things from these beginnings—which are Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury. 

<margin>The principles are the medium between the elements and the metals. <note>original: "Principia sunt medium inter elementa & metalla."</note></margin>

Nature does not simply make a metal directly out of the elements, but rather through these beginnings, which are a medium between the elements and the metals. Therefore, if Nature does not do it...

<summary>This page introduces the "Concluding Speech," outlining the hierarchical process of creation where God creates elements from Chaos, and Nature then produces the "Three Principles" (Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury) as a bridge to metals.</summary>
<keywords>Four Elements, Three Principles, Tria Prima, Salt, Sulfur, Mercury, Chaos, Nature vs. Art, Alchemical Theory</keywords>

173

# Concluding Speech.

[Nature] does, much less will the Art <note>The "Art" refers to alchemy, the human imitation and perfection of natural processes.</note> be able to do so. And not only in this example, but in all natural processes, the intermediate disposition <note>original: "mittelere disposition." This refers to the transitional state or arrangement of matter as it moves from one form to another.</note> must be observed. Therefore, in this treatise, we have described both the elements themselves and their actions and effects extensively enough (such that no philosopher until this day has provided it more clearly), so that a diligent researcher can all the more easily consider in what degree the Stone <note>The Philosopher's Stone, the ultimate goal of the alchemical Great Work.</note> differs from the metals, and the metals from the elements.

We have not written this treatise as if we wished to rebuke the ancient Sages, but rather so that we might confirm their writings and fulfill what they left out; for even the philosophers are men, and cannot do everything perfectly <note>original: "nach der Schnur," literally "according to the string," an idiom for doing something with absolute precision or by the book.</note>, nor is every person sufficient in everything.

Some have also attributed miracles to the direct paths of Nature, as is said to have happened to Albertus Magnus <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280) was a Dominican friar and polymath often associated with alchemical legends.</note>, an excellent man and ingenious philosopher, who wrote that in his time small grains of gold were found between the teeth of a skull in a grave. He could not fathom the actual causes of this wonder, but instead attributed it to a mineral power <term>mineralische Kraft</term> <note>The belief that a "metallic seed" or mineralizing force could exist within biological life, allowing metals to grow in unusual places.</note> within the human being; and for this opinion, the saying of Morienus <note>Morienus Romanus, a legendary 7th-century alchemist said to have taught the Ummayad prince Khalid ibn Yazid.</note> occurred to him as proof, where he says: "And the matter, O King, is taken from you—"
->taken<-

<summary>The author concludes the treatise by explaining the necessity of understanding the transition from elements to metals and acknowledges that even great philosophers like Albertus Magnus had limitations in their understanding of natural miracles.</summary>

[extracted] and drawn out. For the mineral power is placed in its own kingdom, as we have distinguished this same Monarchy <note>Alchemists often used the term "Monarchy" or "Kingdom" to describe the three distinct domains of nature: Animal, Vegetable, and Mineral.</note> in the twelve small treatises and divided it into three kingdoms, since each of these exists and multiplies within itself without the addition of anything foreign. It is indeed true that in the animal kingdom there is also a Mercury <note>original: "Mercurius." In this context, Mercury refers to the "passive" or "material" principle of a substance, not just the metal quicksilver.</note>, acting as a matter, and a Sulphur <note>original: "Schwefel." Sulphur represents the "active" or "soul" principle that gives form and power.</note>, acting as the power; but these are animal, not mineral. If there were no sulphurous animal power in humans, then Mercury could not coagulate <term>coagulate: to thicken or solidify a liquid into a structured form</term> the blood into flesh and bones. In the same way, if there were not a sulphurous vegetable power in plants, the water would not be coagulated, nor would the vegetable Mercury become herbs and trees.

This is also how it should be understood in the mineral kingdom. Indeed, these three Mercuries are not different in their power and virtue, nor are the three Sulphurs; for every Sulphur has a natural power to coagulate its own Mercury, and every Mercury has the power to be coagulated by its own Sulphur, but not by a foreign one.

As for the reason why gold might be found and generated between the teeth of a dead person, the cause is this: during the deceased's lifetime, a physician introduced Mercury into the sick body, either through ointments <note>This refers to "mercurial inunctions," a common but toxic historical treatment for diseases like syphilis.</note>, or by ingestion, or in other ways as is common and customary. Concluding Speech.                                     175

...that it rises to the patient's mouth and is cast out through the mouth along with the saliva. <note>The author is describing the "salivation cure," a common but dangerous medical practice where mercury was administered to patients until they began to drool excessively, a sign the "poison" was leaving the body.</note> If the patient dies during such a treatment, the mercury finds no exit and remains between the teeth. The body then becomes its natural vessel <term>vessel: original "Geſchirr," referring to an alchemical flask or container used for heating and transformation</term>, in which it remains enclosed for a long time. It is then turned into gold by its own sulphur <note>original: "Schwefel." Alchemists believed mercury contained an internal "sulphur" or soul that could solidify it into gold under the right conditions.</note>, after being purified from the sharp, corrosive phlegm of the human body by the natural heat of putrefaction <term>putrefaction: the process of decay, which alchemists viewed as a necessary stage of purification and rebirth</term>. 

But if no mineral mercury had been brought there, gold would never have grown. And this is a true example showing that nature, in the crevices of the earth, produces gold and silver from mercury alone—as well as other metals—depending on the nature of the place or the "mother" <note>original: "Muter," referring to the "womb" of the earth or the geological matrix in which minerals were thought to mature like embryos.</note>. Because mercury carries its own sulphur with it and within it, through which it is coagulated <term>coagulate: to solidify a liquid into a structured form</term> into gold, provided it is not hindered by chance, lacks the proper heat, or is not in an enclosed place. 

Therefore, the power of animal sulphur cannot coagulate mercury into gold, but only into flesh; for if such a power were within humans, it would happen in all bodies, which is not the case. Likewise, many miracles and wonders occur which, if not properly considered by writers, lead readers into error. But an understanding researcher should judge everything according to the possibilities of nature: so it [continues...]

<margin>Sulfur is the coagulation of Mercury. <note>original Latin: "Sulphur est Mercurii Coagulum."</note></margin>

But Sulphur is the hardening or rennet <term>rennet: original "lipp," a substance used to curdle milk; here used metaphorically to describe the agent that solidifies liquid mercury into a solid metal</term> of Mercury: which Sulphur is indeed prepared with great toil, and found with even more difficulty, for in the Sulphur of the Wise <note>The "Sulphur of the Wise" refers to "Philosophical Sulphur," a purified spiritual principle sought by alchemists, rather than common brimstone.</note> lies the entire secret, which is also found in the innermost part of Mercury. Regarding its preparation (without which it is useless), we shall treat of that in the third treatise, concerning Salt; but here we speak of the origin and the power of Sulphur.

In Mercury is everything that the Wise seek.

In me, Mercury, all is hidden,

I carry with me Water and Fire,

If you find it, keep it secret.

178 Conversation of the Spirit of Mercury

# Conversation of the Spirit of Mercury / With Brother Albert Bayrs / A Carmelite
# Monk, etc.

### Mercury.

[Decorative initial W]hat is the reason that you have enchanted and bound me with so much idolatry and conjurations? <note>"Beschwerungen" here refers to the ritualistic "binding" or "burdening" of a spirit through magical commands.</note>

### Albertus.

I will tell you the reason, if you will first assure me against danger to my body, my life, and my soul.

### Mercury.

That is not within my power. However, I have not come to do such things to you. But if you will not cease from your sorcery, you are already assigned to another, who will well know how to carry out the execution <note>Meaning the final punishment or judgment.</note> upon you and your kind. Regarding the salvation of your soul, I can neither hinder nor help you; yet, if I were a human being, I would certainly wish to be saved. Therefore, answer my question.

### Albertus.

I beg of you, do not be angry with me, for I am a dull <note>original: "blöder." In this context, it suggests a "simple" or "frail" mortal capacity compared to a spirit.</note> human, but you are a swift and powerful spirit. Therefore, tell me first: are you a good or an evil angel, or who are you?

### Mercury.

<summary>This page depicts a dramatic dialogue where the Spirit of Mercury confronts Brother Albert Bayrs for using magic to summon him. Mercury warns the monk that while he cannot harm the soul, the monk’s practice of sorcery risks divine or demonic punishment.</summary>
<keywords>Mercury, Albert Bayrs, Carmelite monk, conjuration, sorcery, salvation, Spirit of Mercury, alchemy dialogue</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Brother Albert Bayrs and the Spirit of Mercury. In the preceding text, Albertus, fearful of the spirit he has summoned through ritual magic, asked whether the entity was a good or evil angel. Here, Mercury begins his explanation of his true nature—not as a moral being (angel or demon), but as a natural, planetary force that mediates between the heavens and the earth.</meta>

->179<-

### With Brother Albert Bayrs.

### Mercury.

I am neither an evil nor a good angel; rather, I am one of the seven planetary spirits who govern middle nature <note>The "middle nature" (mittel Natur) refers to the realm between the purely divine/spiritual and the purely physical/material world.</note>. These spirits are commanded to rule the four distinct parts of the world: namely the firmamental <note>The heavens or celestial sphere.</note>, the animal, the vegetable, and the mineral parts. There are seven of us who, through our skill, lead all earthly powers and the influences of the Upper Circle <note>The celestial or divine realm.</note> into the three lower parts through the ascendants and descendants <note>Terms from astrology referring to the rising and falling of celestial bodies, which were believed to transmit power to Earth.</note>, and we work within them; for the planets can never descend physically. 

<note>original NB: "daß ist d Gaist..." Note: This is the spirit that effectively helps to bring forth the internal, generative creatures from the creation of the four elements; and whoever understands this will be prepared for the Work <term>The Work: In alchemy, "the Work" (the Great Work or *Magnum Opus*) refers to the process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</term>.</note>

### Albertus.

I am entirely gladdened by your high spiritual report. I acknowledge with all my heart the pure truth: that I perceive more fundamental ground in your very clear report than I have found in all the philosophers <note>original: "Philosophis." In this context, Albertus refers to the "Natural Philosophers" or alchemical authorities of the past.</note> until now. But I beg of you, grant me one more question, and then I will tell you the reason why I have summoned you and declare it to you in an orderly way: I pray you, tell me your name.

### Mercury.

I am called and I am the Spirit of the Planet, and not of the God Mercury. You have not brought me to you through your bans and conjurations; rather, I have come to you quite voluntarily through God's permission. Therefore...

[Signature mark:] N ij [Catchword:] ...me

Albertus expresses that this revelation is more profound than any alchemical text he has previously studied.</summary>

neither your circles, candles, nor swords, nor your other fantasies concern me in the least; for regardless of these, a serving spirit is granted by God to every pious person, yet one finds few who make themselves worthy of such a gift. Therefore, be no longer terrified by my blackness, for it shall be a beginning of your wealth <note>The "blackness" refers to the *nigredo* phase of alchemy, where matter must first be "killed" or decomposed—turning black—before it can be purified and transformed into gold.</note>. For in the beginning of Creation, everything was also dark and obscure; but through the wisdom of the Creator, the light was separated from the darkness. For after the lovely dawn <note>original: *Morgenröth*. In alchemy, the "Aurora" or dawn represents the transition from the black stage to the red stage.</note>, the sun rises quite beautifully—high, sanguine <note>A deep, blood-red color.</note>, and fiery red. If you now believe my words—though they are not human, but rather a sounding echo suited to my nature—I will hear you kindly and instruct you. Mercury is telling him that such protections are unnecessary for a natural spirit.</note> and let me enter; sit at the table and write with diligence what I shall tell you. But you, begin with the reasons why you have thus summoned me and desired me; and do not be overly curious, but rather plain and brief in your questions.

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the alchemist Brother Albertus Bahrs and the Spirit of Mercury. Following Mercury's command on the previous page to set aside magical circles and sit at a table to record his teachings, Albertus now begins his formal inquiry into the physical reality of alchemical transmutation.</meta>

181
With Brother Albertus Bahrs.

Whether what the **Philosophers** have written of their **Stone of the Philosophers** or **Tincture** <note>In alchemy, the "Tincture" is a potent liquid or powder—often synonymous with the Philosopher's Stone—capable of "tinging" or transforming base metals into gold and healing the human body.</note> is true in the nature of things <note>original Latin: *in rerum natura*</note>, or if it is merely a subtle speculation?

### Mercury.

Know that the philosophers have written many things about this one single thing with great caution, so that fools—who only ask and strive for money—and unfaithful, arrogant men might be led astray. Thus, the high secrets of Nature (which are the natural powers that accomplish all things), for which many people of high and low status strive, may remain all the more secret. But they have not told, nor can they tell, the truth in any other way except regarding one single thing (which is "all in all"); everything else serves more to lead the unworthy astray. Therefore, I tell you the pure truth in few words: that when they have written in concordance <note>original: *Concordantz*. This refers to the tradition of "Concordantia Philosophorum," where scholars compared different alchemical texts to find the underlying truth hidden beneath varied metaphors.</note> about their **Stone** or **Tincture**, it is true and certain in the nature of things.

### Albertus.

What is that same single thing?

### Mercury.

You, as a well-read scholar <note>original: *Sophist*. While today this implies trickery, in this historical context it refers to someone trained in formal logic and academic rhetoric.</note> and practiced laboratory worker, should at least have learned from your Bernard <note>A reference to Bernard of Trevisan (1406–1490), a famous alchemist whose works were highly influential. His "double Mercury" refers to the marriage of the "fixed" and the "volatile" aspects of the substance.</note>, as you let yourself believe you know his double **Spirit of Mercury**...

R iij <column-break/>

<summary>Albertus asks the Spirit of Mercury whether the Philosopher's Stone is a physical reality; Mercury confirms its existence but explains that alchemists purposefully use deceptive language to protect the secret from the greedy.</summary>
<keywords>Philosopher's Stone, Tincture, Bernard of Trevisan, Spirit of Mercury, alchemical secrecy, nature of things, alchemy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the Dialogue of the Spirit of Mercury. On the previous page, Mercury began to explain that the "Stone" described by the philosophers is a real, physical possibility, though hidden behind allegories to keep it from the unworthy. Mercury is currently addressing Albertus's previous academic studies, contrasting theoretical knowledge with true alchemical insight.</meta>

182 ->Dialogue of the Spirit of Mercury<-

...very well, and you have speculated yourself nearly into a fool regarding the **First Entity** <note>original Latin: *primo Ente*. This refers to the "Prima Materia" or the original, formless matter from which all things are created.</note> and your **Azoth** <note>Azoth was considered by alchemists to be the "universal solvent" or the "essential agent" of transformation, often symbolized by the caduceus or a dragon.</note>, yet you are still very far from the true Center. This is because you seek life among the dead, and you look for the most stable and indestructible strength—the strongest of all natural strengths—within unstable and perishable things.

Therefore, know the truth: that our **tincture** <note>In alchemy, a tincture is a potent substance capable of "tinging" or transmuting base metals into gold or silver.</note> becomes entirely red and pure when it is extracted from the most perfect creature upon which the Sun has ever shone. This one thing is joined together so compactly through the most stable Spirits, the composition of the 4 different Qualities or **Elements** <note>The four classical elements: Earth, Air, Fire, and Water.</note>, and the **Concordance of the 7 Stars** <note>This refers to the seven classical planets—Sun, Moon, Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn—which alchemists believed governed the seven corresponding metals on Earth.</note>. It is brought to its degree of perfection without any human intervention, help, or art. Furthermore, it is so naturally gifted in its creation with an unbelievable multiplication of its own seed and offspring that its parts are firmly bound together; thus, it cannot be destroyed or harmed by any natural element without the aid of art. 

Since, outside of this one thing, all other natural things are subject to **Corruption** <note>In a philosophical sense, "corruption" refers to the natural process of decay, breaking down, or changing from a higher state to a lower one.</note>. Let this be enough of a report for you for now regarding the kind of matter from which the **Philosophers** have extracted their tinctures. **NB.** <note>An abbreviation for the Latin *Nota Bene*, meaning "Note Well" or "Pay Attention."</note> If you understand or recognize what is contained in these few words, then you understand the entire business and the **Sum of the Art**. Indeed, to whom God opens the eyes, enough has been said here.
->enough has been<-
->said.<-

<summary>The Spirit of Mercury rebukes Albertus for seeking the eternal Philosopher's Stone in perishable materials, explaining that the true Tincture is a perfectly balanced substance composed of the four elements and influenced by the seven planets.</summary>
<keywords>First Entity, Azoth, Tincture, Four Elements, Seven Stars, Corruption, Sum of the Art, alchemy, transmutation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the Spirit of Mercury and Brother Albertus Bayrs. On the previous page, Mercury described a "tincture" of perfect purity, extracted from a creature superior to all others, formed by the elements and the stars without human intervention. This page clarifies that this "creature" is a type of gold, but not the common metal found in a forge.</meta>

183
->With Brother Albertus Bayrs.<-

...said. It might also be interpreted as referring to gold, but that is not understood correctly; for there are even higher created **creatures** <note>In early modern alchemy, "creatures" referred to any created thing in nature, including minerals and metals, not just living animals.</note> than gold. One must search for these, and then the truth will be found: that which God has placed in Nature, which man refuses to recognize even if it were written right under his nose. It is, therefore, not grasped because of man's great blindness and his lack of self-knowledge.

### Albertus

I understand from your obscure report that you mean fine gold.

### Mercurius

You have partly understood correctly, but a dark cloud still floats before your eyes. It is indeed the finest gold, but not that which is found in the melting furnace. Rather, it is that which Nature herself has refined through her **volcanic Archeus** <note>original: *Vulcaniſchen Archeum*. In the tradition of Paracelsus, the *Archeus* is the "inner alchemist" or vital force that directs the growth and purification of substances within the earth or the body.</note> without any help from **Art** <note>Alchemists used the term "Art" to refer to human technology, chemistry, and craft, often contrasting it with the superior "Work of Nature."</note>, in her own way. From this is drawn that same **double Mercury** <note>Also known as the "Mercury of the Philosophers," this is a specialized substance believed to contain both "fixed" and "volatile" principles necessary for the Great Work.</note>. When you possess that, then argue with your Abbot and say: **Azoth and fire are sufficient for you.** <note>original Latin: *Azoth et ignis tibi ſufficiunt*. Azoth is the "universal solvent" or essential spirit of transformation; fire provides the energy for the change.</note> 

**NB.** <note>Latin abbreviation for *nota bene*, meaning "pay close attention."</note> It is obvious that this is more than the finest gold, for God himself commanded it in the creation and granted it this power to reveal itself to us humans. Through this, then, all people can possess such things if they are rightly enlightened by God.

N iiii Albertus

<summary>Mercury clarifies that the "gold" of the philosophers is not the common metal refined by human hands, but a superior substance purified by Nature's own internal fire (the Archeus). He introduces the concept of the "double Mercury" and the maxim that "Azoth and fire" are the only essential requirements for the alchemical work.</summary>
<keywords>Archeus, Azoth, double Mercury, Nature vs. Art, Albertus Bayrs, volcanic fire, enlightenment</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the Spirit of Mercury and Brother Albertus Bayrs from page 183. Having established that the "gold" they seek is a natural product of the earth rather than the result of a furnace, they now discuss its location, the quantity required, and the distinction between its physical form and its spiritual essence.</meta>

184 . Dialogue of Mercury

**Albertus.**

Yes, where then does one obtain this same gold?

**Mercurius.**

Under the heavens, in many mountains and mines. <note>original: *NB. Alle Menſchen habens vor Augen/ vnd kennen das nicht.* All people have it before their eyes, and do not recognize it.</note>

**Albertus.**

How much must one have for the completion of this work?

**Mercurius.**

If you have four **lots** <note>A *Loth* was a pre-metric unit of weight common in German-speaking lands, roughly equivalent to 14.5 to 17 grams, or about half an ounce.</note>, you might buy the crowns from the Pope and keep the rest.

**Albertus.**

We shall certainly manage to obtain that much with God’s help. <note>original: *NB. Wenn du 4. loth außgearbeitet/ ſo iſt es genug zu deinem anfang.* When you have prepared 4 lots, it is enough for your beginning.</note>

**Mercurius.**

Yes, the **body** <note>original: *Corpus*</note>. But do you not know that I, as a spirit, speak not of the body, but much more of the **spirit** <note>original: *Spiritu*</note>? How do you wish to weigh the spirit, which is extracted in such a slight and small quantity from its body, but afterwards in its **potency** <note>original: *virtute*; in alchemical philosophy, the *virtue* or power of a substance is its concentrated active principle, which is far more effective than the raw bulk material.</note> surpasses the great quantity of its body? If you now wish to make this extracted spirit **physical** <note>original: *Corporaliſch*</note> again through itself, and to a spiritual pure—

<summary>Mercury explains that the "philosophical gold" is hidden in plain sight throughout nature and emphasizes that the true work involves the spiritual essence (Spiritus) rather than the mere physical bulk (Corpus) of the material.</summary>
<keywords>Mercury, Albertus Bayrs, Loth, weight, Corpus, Spiritus, Virtute, mines, alchemy of nature</keywords>

--- Page 211 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the Spirit of Mercury and Brother Albertus Bayrs. On the previous page, Mercury was explaining that the alchemist must first extract a spirit from its body and then return that spirit into a physical, purified form.</meta>

### With Brother Albertus Bayrs. 185

...to transform into a body, then you may debate with your Abbot (though beforehand it is unnecessary) and say: "fire and **Azoth** are sufficient for you." <note>original: *ignis et Azoth tibi sufficiunt*. In alchemical theory, "Azoth" was considered the universal solvent or the essential life force of matter, while fire provided the energy for transformation.</note>

**Albertus.**

Oh angelic, oh heavenly words! How then shall I go about it?

**Mercurius.**

Dissolve and congeal. <note>original: *Solve et coagula*. This is the most famous maxim in alchemy, referring to the process of breaking a substance down to its base elements (dissolving) and then bringing them back together in a purified state (congealing).</note> <margin>that is: congeal, dissolve, and congeal <note>original: *id est coagula, Solve, et coagula*</note></margin>

**Albertus.**

Oh, those are short words that are difficult to understand, yet the entire Art is contained within them! I understand: I must dissolve the **body of the sun** <term>corpus solis; in alchemical shorthand, the "Sun" (Sol) refers to gold.</term> and, through that dissolution, extract the **dyeing spirit** <note>original: *spiritum tingentem*; the active, spiritual essence of a substance that has the power to "tinge" or transform other metals.</note> which is, without doubt, the "double Mercury" of Bernhardus. <note>Refers to Bernard of Trevisan, a 15th-century Italian alchemist whose writings on the dual nature of Mercury—being both a metal and a spirit—were highly influential.</note> **NB.** <note>Latin abbreviation for *nota bene*, meaning "note well."</note> The Body is not fine gold, but that in which the **tincture** <term>A "tincture" is the concentrated medicinal or transformative essence sought by alchemists.</term> lies hidden; from that, draw out the double Mercury.

**Mercurius.**

Now the veil is partly removed from your eyes; you have understood it correctly. **NB.** Mark well now which "body" he means.

**Albertus.**

By what means must I dissolve the body of the sun?

**Mercurius.**

By itself, and by that which is most closely related to it.

-> N v Alber- <- <note>This is a "catchword," a common feature in old books where the first word of the following page is printed at the bottom of the current one to help the binder arrange the pages correctly.</note>

<summary>Mercury reveals the central alchemical maxim "Solve et Coagula," leading Albertus to realize that the source of the "dyeing spirit" is not common gold, but a specific related substance where the tincture lies hidden.</summary>
<keywords>Solve et Coagula, Azoth, Body of the Sun, Tincture, Bernhardus Trevisanus, Double Mercury, Alchemical Dissolution</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the Spirit of Mercury and Brother Albertus Bayrs. While the previous page provided was numbered 185, this transcription is labeled 184; it continues the technical discussion regarding the preparation of the alchemical "King" and the "Fountain."</meta>

184 ->Dialogue of the Spirit of Mercury<-

->Albertus.<-

That is a difficult statement—indeed, more difficult than the Art itself. I pray you, explain it to me and show me the means and the practical methods of the true **solution**. <note>original: *solution*; in alchemy, this refers to the process of dissolving a solid into a liquid state so it can be purified.</note>

->Mercurius.<-

I, being a spirit, cannot show you the means and the practical methods right now, for I have no hands. If I had a body like yours, I would perform the whole work myself. You, however, must search diligently in your **Bernhardus**, <note>A reference to the writings of Bernard of Trevisan, a famous 15th-century alchemist whose allegories were central to the "Great Work."</note> wherein are the means and the methods of the true solution described three times in all their details—twice correctly and once falsely for the sake of the unworthy. <note>It was a common practice for alchemical authors to include "blinds" or false instructions to ensure their secrets were only discovered by the virtuous and persistent.</note>

->Albertus.<-

Alas, miserable as I am, I have already almost read myself to death in those pages, yet I still cannot find it. For although I know the **King** <term>The "King" usually represents gold or the fixed, perfected matter at the heart of the process.</term> through your instruction, the **Fountain** <note>original: *Fontina*; likely referring to the "Fountain of Bernard," a famous allegory where a king must bathe in a specific solvent to be cleansed and reborn.</note> mentioned therein remains entirely unknown to me. Therefore, I beg you most earnestly: show me what the Fountain is.

->Mercurius.<-

You wish to become all too learned all too soon. I cannot show it to you; you must first possess the King. One does not heat the bath until the King is at hand. <note>This suggests that the preparation of the material to be transformed must be completed before the solvent or "bath" is prepared.</note> Instead, go to your Abbot and tell him he should procure for you ten pounds of the best Oriental **Mercury** <note>The symbol ☿ represents Mercury or quicksilver.</note> [8 7 5 6. scruple, s, Aleph] <note>The transcription includes symbols for weights and the Hebrew letter Aleph (ℵ), which often signified the "First Matter" or the beginning of the work. The symbols ℈ (scruple) and others relate to specific pharmaceutical or alchemical measurements.</note> just as it comes out of its mother, the Earth, without the use of fire. <note>Mercury "without fire" refers to "native" or "virgin" mercury found naturally in the earth, rather than mercury extracted through the heating and smelting of cinnabar ore.</note> Then I will...

[afterward—]

<summary>In this dialogue, the Spirit of Mercury refuses to reveal the final practical steps to Albertus, instructing him instead to find the "Fountain" in the writings of Bernard of Trevisan and to procure pure, "virgin" mercury from his Abbot.</summary>
<keywords>Spirit of Mercury, Albertus Bayrs, Bernard of Trevisan, alchemical solution, The King, The Fountain, virgin mercury, alchemical symbols</keywords>
